No. 7.

the corselet skirt becomes—as is almost certain to be Ithe case—popular. In the bands of a highly skilled modiste, the idea is smart, but, further, it needs soft i yielding fabrics, exquisite colouring, and a perfect ' figure, to make it an unqualified success. So far, the "braces "have been invisible, hidden beneath the folds of the blouse, bat the latest notion is to build them of some handsome trimming and allow them to show. ' About Mourning.—Even the deepest mourning is smart now-a-days, and the woman who is dowdy in her sable attire is so from choice, and not from necessity. In Paris, white crepe is having an immense vogue, for quite deep mourning, jet being the trimming preferred. Jet ornaments too, are in high favour among fashion- able Parisiennes, the tiaras and collars being veritable works of art. The former are built in the Empire and Louis XVI. designs.and often combined with paste. In donning white as a sign of mourning, the Parisiennes are but following the custom of the Chinese. In Spain too, the same custom obtained until the year 1498. In Turkey violet is the mourning colour, in Egypt yellow, and in Ethiopia.brown. Children Fashionable.—Children are well to the fore just now, indeed they are, as a Society journal rather bluntly put it the other day—fashionable. The Duchess of Marlborough is seen about a great deal with her two handsome boys, while the little Lady Ursula Grosvenor is quite a companion to her mother, the Duchess of Westminster. Lady Exeter, too, takes her small daughter about a great deal, and the same may be said of Lady Castlereagh, Lady Granby. Lady Essex, Lady Scarborough, the Hon. Mrs. George Keppel, and many other Society ladies. These gay little people walk with their mothers in the morning, drive with them in the ' afternoon, and are taken to all kinds of entertainments, not infrequently winding up with tea at a fashionable restaurant, where they hold quite a little Court. ===== 0002601_19060224_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

--- The Social%ide of Politics.—There has been a great deal of social and political entertaining, prior to the opening of Parliament, Lady Campbell-Bannerman, and Lady Lansdowne, being conspicuous among the grandes dames who figure as political hostesses. Lady Wim- borne was to have given a big dinner and reception in honour of the new regime, but this had to be postponed in consequence of the sad death of her sister Lady Howe, which has placed several noble families in mourning. They were opposed to one another in politics,for whereas Lady Wimhorne has fully embraced the Liberal faith, like her other sister—the late Lady Tweedmouth,Lady Howe on the other hand was a staunch Conservative. She had many of the oratorial gifts of her brother, Lord Randolph Churchill, and until her illness in 1902 she often appeared on the public platfotm on behalf of her party. In the near future however, Lady Wimborne will be one of the prominent hostesses on the Government side and Lord Wimborne's fine mansion in London, which was formerly the family residence of the Dukes of Hamilton, is well fitted for entertainments on a large scale. Another Romance of the Stage and Peerage.—Lord de Clifford, who has followed the example of the Earl of Orkney, Lord Clancarty, the Marquess of Headfort and the Earl of Clonmell, by marrying an actress, comes from one of the oldest families in the peerage, and owns a large estate in County Mayo, but his seat, Dalgan Park, is in County Galway, near Tuam. He was left an orphan when only ten years of age and has been brought up by his aunts, the Hon. Maud Russell and her sisters. Lord de Clifford is a thorough sportsman, and is now in his twenty-second year, a few months older than his wife, who was Miss Eva Carrington, recently appearing in "Bluebell "at a London theatre. She has been several years on the stage, and first met Lord de Clifford last autumn at Dublin, where she was appearing in "The Catch of the Season." No one suspected that the incident had any significance until last week, when, Lord de Clifford after returning from abroad, proposed and married this charming young lady, whose love story is as romantic and idyllic as the most sentimental young maiden could desire. _ _

A Former Romance of the Stage.—The Countess of Orkney, who was Miss Connie Gilchrist, of the Gaiety Theatre is seldom seen in London, as she and her husband spend most of their time in Ireland. Their other place is in Bucks, as, though the Earl bears a Scottish title he does not own any land in Scotland. Both Lord and Lady Orkney are fearless riders, and have often come to grief in the hunting field. So also has Lord de Clifford, who is the youngest master of foxhounds in the country. Lord and Lady Orkney have a little daughter some two or three years old, and, being an only child, she is an important little lady, as she is heiress presumptive to the earldom. Marrid Women and their Maiden Name.—The American girl is noted for her independent spirit and in the United States there is a growing tendency for the married woman to retain her maiden name after marriage. In this country it is never done. A married woman seldom hears her maiden name mentioned and often it is quite unknown to many of her dearest friends. In France on the contrary, aud also in some other continental countries, marriage does not so entirely extinguish it, and many married women combine the old name with the new. It is stated that President Roosevelts' daughter, whose marriage last week was the occasion eta national celebration in America will adopt this course, and have her visiting cards inscribed Mrs. Longworth-Roosevelt. American Trousseaux.—The Duchess of Marlborough ordered, and completed the trousseau in five weeks, a feat deemed wonderful even in America. But Miss Roosevelt, or Mrs. Longworth, as we must callher now, gave only three weeks to this important undertaking, the United States supplying the majority of the fabrics, and New York and Washington the workmanship. Many beautiful lengths of material, collected or presented to her during her recent tour, Mrs. Longworth has utilized of course. First and foremost, are the filmy pineapple gauzes of the Phillipines, and a delicate cloth named iusi, a production of the native women of these islands. Then there are the handsome and Japanese silks, which have been principally used for " rest " robes ; gold wrought, and flower-strewn fabrics that have been built up into opera cloaks and coats, embroidered crepe de chine and Oriental silks, that have been finished into Princess robes, for which by the way, Mrs. Longworth has a decided penchant. All walking dresses in this famous and extensive trousseau are quite three inches- off the ground, and, as far as possible, Mrs. Longworth has decided throughout in favour of simplicity rather than'elabora t ion. Trimmings and Embroideries.—Apparently this is to be a season of rich and costly trimmings. There are most beautiful hand-made embroideries in gold and old- , world colourings, silver and pearl, while yet mixed with gold, and yet mixed with silver, form some of the loveliest trimmings in evidence. Even quite short lengths of these embroideries, help to raise considerably the sum total of a gown, and many of them are quite incapablelof resisting the faintest suspicion of rain or damp. Unless possessed of a generous dress allowance, it is therefore wiser to avoid anything in the way of elaborate embroideries, or trimmings. The good ones are quite beyond the moderately endowed and tawdry, cheap imitations are always in the very worst of style and taste. _

A TWICE TOLD TALE.

Good news bears repeating, and when it is confirmed after ajong lapse of time by the giver of it, even if we hesitated to believe it at first hearing, we feel secure in accepting its truth now. The following marvellous experience of a Newton-le-Willows woman is confirmed after four years. "Four years ago Doan's backache kidney pills cured me of kidney trouble, and I have had no return of it," says Mrs. E. Cooke, 45, Golborne Dale-road, Newton-le- Willows, near Warrington. "I have been so well during the last twelve months that I have not needed to take any of the pills at all. I take a pleasure in recommending the medicine to my friends." The following is the letter Mrs. Cooke sent to us some years ago:—" Dear sirs,—For some time I was subject to acute pains in my back and stomach, and although I tried different treatments, I could get no relief. "One day I happened to read about Doan's Backache Kidney Pills., and I sent for some to try. I have been a great deal better since using this medicine, and my back has been much easier. If the pains return I shall take some more of Doan's pills." Doan's Backache Kidney Pills are two shillings and ninepence per box (six boxes for thirteen shillings and ninepence). Of all chemists and stores, or post free, diregt from Foster-McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, Oxford-street, London, W. You are sure to get the right medicine if you ask distinctly for Doan's.

We have just had submitted to us a copy of an attrac- tive booklet issued by the Great Western Railway Com- pany as a preliminary announcement of the facilities which will be afforded in connection with the new direct route, via Fishguard, between England and Ireland to be inaugurated this summer. Fishguard Harbour, situated in South Cardigan Bay, will be the Port of Call on this side of St. George' Channel. This Harbour which is very favonrsbly situated, affords spacious and safe anchorage in deep water ; and extensive Quay accommodation for dealing with Passenger and Goods traffic has been provided. It is proposed to run a day and night service in each direction, and through rail communication with all parts of England will be established by moans of express trains which will run direct to and from the Quay at Fishguard, thus greatly facilitating the landing and embarkation of passengers at that port. For the cross- channel service magnificently appointed turbine steamers are in course of construction. They will be capable of a speed of 221: knots and will therefore accomplish the passage of 54 nautical miles between Fishguard and Rosslare, the port on the Irish side, well under three hours, thus providing the shortest sea route between England and Ireland. These express steamers will be fitted with commodious refrigerating chambers for the transport of perishable traffic. Reference is made in this publication to the fact that, on a recent occasion, when the Cunard liner "Carmania " was unable, through stress of weather, to reach Queenstown with the passengers and mails, many vessels naltde Fishguard Harbour in safety, and remained in shelter there for some time. This of course points to the possibility of Fishguard as a port of call for ocean liners.

The King will hold a Council on Tuesday next, the 27th inst. The death has taken place in Ceylon of Mr. William Barker Ogden, the well-known tobacco manufacturer.

CHAPTER X:

LADY MARY JARDINI only returned from York in time for dinner; she was so full of her visit and her friends that she forgot to ques- tion Helen of her own doings, and talked enough for both of them ,so • that the girl's silence passed unnoticed. " I am sure you are better, Helen," she said when tea was brought to them in the drawing- room; "you have quite your, own colour to- night, and your eyes are bright instead of being dull and heavy as they have looked lately." • She was a good woman but utterly tinversed in the book of the human heart; she had no idea the pink colour in Helen's cheeks and the vived brightness of her eyes were due to excitement. The girl's whole being was worked up to fever pitch in her intense long- ing to know if Lord Alandale had kept his in- tention of seeing Mr. Norton ' and what had come of the interview. Lady Mary was tired with her exertions and went to bed early. Helen retired at the same, time because she never liked being alone after dark in the vast reception rooms; she did not expect to sleep, her anxiety was far too acute. On her dressing table lay a little note of the kind which had grown so painfully familiar to her in the last few months. She dissuissed her maid hurriedly; it might cont aie a de- mand for a meeting, and what was the 3se of undressing if she had to go out. She sat for some minutes motionlest after the maid had left her. In her hand wa.; the note Which might answer the question which so troubled her, but she shrank from opening 4t. What if Richard had told Lord Alandale the real nature of the tie which bound her to him. It 'seemed to Helen that if Kenneth ever knew that she could never look into his face again. At last she brought her courage to the point and tore open the note ; it was very short and told her nothing definite. "Proposal made me by friend of yours Must know if you endorse it, and if you under. stand terms only binding till your next birth. day. Meet me to-night to settle things. D." She burnt the note in her candle, just as she had burnt every communication from Mrs Martin's lodger. Then reluctantly she took out her cloak and hat. She dared not refuse to meet Dick Norton, and yet she hated to do so this night more than she had ever done before. Kenneth liad begged her not to see the man again but to leave everything to him. In going out to-night she was thwarting the wish of the man dearest to her on earth. ' "It was for the last time," that was the thought with which she tried to comfort her- self as she made her way to the trysting place under the great cedar. Whether Norton ac- cepted the Earl's terms or not, she would never steal forth from her home to meet him by night again, rather let' him do his worst. She was weary of the struggle. To her surprise Dick seemed far more amiable than she • had expected to find lima. Perhaps he had come to regard a hundred pounds in hard cash as better value than ten pounds a month, extorted with much diffi- culty from a reluctant victim. He told Helen civilly enough that he would take the Earl's hundred pounds and leave Yorkshire for six months, but that he would return as soon as her birthday came. "And then," he concluded, "you'll have to make your choice, either let me reign at Alan- dale Hall as Prince Consort or I'll expose your'. fraud." "You will gain nothing," she muttered. "That's all you know. I've made en- quiries and I find the real heiress is a girl living with her mother in poor circumstances com- pared with what she ought to have. The mother, I hear, loves money beyond every- thing. Surely you can see my game. If you drive me to it I'll take my story to Mrs. Charles Jardine and sell it for a price. You'll have six months to make up your mind," 'he went on, "but just remember this, I mean every word I say." To her life's end Helen never forgot that moment, a surging flood of divers feelings rushed through her mind, she hated the man before her as she had never in her life hated human being, and yet she could not forget the time when she had believed in him, when she had thought him brave enough to conquer his failings and make of himself a man she might trust

No sound escaped her, she was not one of thoso women who feel relief in expressing their woes in words, no reproaches she could speak to Dick would undo the pass, so she turfed on her heel and left him in perfect silence. She was glad to think afterwards that she had spared him the bitter taunt which tose to her lips. She wrapped her cloak more closely round her and walked rapidly towards the hall. A great terror seized on her; she could not have told of what she felt afraid, but the fear was there, the tall trees with their gaunt shadows seemed like some grim ogres in her path ; she hurried on panting, breathless, and as she went Q seemed to her that she heard the re- port of fire arms whizzing through the air. She told herself it was but the fancy of her harassed brain. .Who would fire a revolver at that time of night. If poachers were abroad they had other means to snare their game. No, the sound she heard was only fog signals, or else existed only in her imagination. It was summer time when fogs are not, and there was but little night traffic on the small country branch line of rail nearest to Alandale Hall, but Helen was in no mood to think of that. " Of course it was fog signals," she told her- self, and yet she hprried forne at her utmost speed, and once safe in bed pulled the clothes -well over her head as though to shut out the awful sound. Six months' respite, six months' peace, would be hers, for from his manner she never doubtEd Dick would accept Lord Alan- dale's hundred pounds. But what could she do when the time of grace expired? After all she was but postponing the evil day when she must decide between exposure and life with Dick. • As she lay awake through the watches of the summer night the strangest idea came to Helen; if worst came to worst and she had to give up .the home she had stolen, the posi- tion she had assumed, she would go for help to the very creature she had most wronged— Magdalen Jardine. Since coming to Alandale Hall she had heard a great' deal of the girl who or years had been looked on as its heiress. At one time she had almost hated Magdalen because "all men spoke well of her," but that phase of feeling had passed, and for weeks now she had thought of the girl she had wronged with a deep remorse. It seemed to Helen that if she went to Kew and poured out her story to Magdalen Jardine, /the elder girl would forgive her and even help her to escape from England and hide Merself in some foreign land where Dick could not pursue her and the memory of her sin might be blotted. out. She slept at lns as peacefully as a child slept till her maid came with the hot water and her early cup of tea. The girl's eyes were round with wonder; her face wore the excited air common to her class when they have some- thing startling to communicate. Susan knew perfectly that her young lady had sometimes gone out into the grounds at night after her aunt believed her to be in bed, but the maid was really attached to her mis- tress, and instead of thinking any harm of these strange nocturnal rambles, she had described them in her own mind to the fact of Miss Jardine being " Colonial." Susan had the most peeuliar ideas of the vast district inhabited by Eaglich speaking people beyond the sea ; she believed that they

"Aunt Mary,", he said very quietly, for he knew how sorely her pride must be wounded, "do not look- so troubled and cast down. We shall soon weather this storm. "No," said Kenneth decidedly, "1 shall never forget your goodness in wishing it, Aunt Mary, but I won't have it said that one of my name ran away. If they want to arrest me they shall come and take me here in my own house."

"You are right". said Mr. Arnot gravely. "I begin to hope though, Kenneth, that the police have thought better of it ; it must be two hours and more since they came to me for the warrant."

" Alanclale shook his head.

" I shall not stir from this house until I go down to the 'Green Man' to be present at the inquest. I expect the real reason of the warrant not being used is that I am to be called as a Witness. Depend upotv it, the police wig keep me in sight till then."

Lord Alandale

"He'd feel it a liberty to hint at the sus- picions abroad against me, and he's too in- nately true to see me and keep them back, so he's taken the easiest course and stly c I awoy " Dr. Graham had not been in the parish very long; he was a clever man and very good to the poor, but he had an almost exaggerated respect for his superiors and a great fear of presuming on their cordiality, so probably Lord Alandale's theory was right. The ladies drove back to the Hall, but Mr. Arnot remained. at Woodlands ; he wanted to say a few words in private to the Earl, es- pecially to tell him of the discovery that Norton was a ticket-of-leave man and the hus- band of Hildred Jasper, the dead girl who had left Australia with Miss Jardine. . WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

ESTIMATES FOR THE YEAR. Increased Call upon the Rates.

The principll item under discussion at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee which was held on Monday was the consideration of the esti- mates. These were presented by Mr. A. J. Jolley as chairman of the Finance Committee and show an increased call from the rates amounting roughly to 21d. in the Z. Alderman Bennett presided and there were also present: —The Mayor, Alderman Smethurst, Conneillors Barton, Ellison. Evans, Sankey,Fulton Smith,Wilkinson, Messrs. W. Bibby, R. T. Fairclough, A. J. Jolley, H. Kingdon, T. May, C. E. Parker, E. Plinston, H. Roberts, C. H. Unsworth, J. Upson, G. Walker, Revs. Canon, Stevenson, N. Wilson, J. Yonge, Messrs. J. Moore Murray (director), and John Fairhurst (treasurer). APPOINTMENTS TO THE NEW COMMITTEE.

--- - The CHAIRMAN said that the first business was to make a recommendation to the Town Council for the appointment of two persons of experience in education, and acquainted with the needs of the various kinds of schools in the borough, as selected members of the Education Committee for the next three years, in accord- and with the scheme made by the Council and approved by the Board of Education. The members were perhaps aware that the three members of the committee ander that head were Mr. Jolley, Mr. May and Mr. Roberts. Mr. Roberts had only been appointed some short time, ance as the appointment was for three years only Messrs. Jolley and May now retired. They were eligible for re-election and he asked for a proposition on the matter. Councillor ELLISON proposed the re-election of the retiring members. Alderman SMETHURST seconded. Mr. PLINSTON supported,and it was unanimously carried Mr. JOLLEY returned thanks on behalf of himself and Mr. May.

THE ESTIMATES. THE INCREASE EXPLAINED.

Mr. JOLLEY proposed the adoption of the estimates. He said the estimates for the coming financial year would have been more trustworthy had they been able to know exactly what the expenditure was for the year just closing,but as there were still six weeks to run they were not able to speak with absolute confidence ; still the figures had been most carefully considered, and he could assure the committee that, when the year did close, they would find that the actual expenditure had been very close to the estimates. The estimates for the School Attendance Sqb-committee were verysimilar to those of the year now closing. The items refarring to the maintenance of children in various institutions. accounted for the increase of £lOO 9s. 4d. The com- mittee provided for the average number of children being sent to the different institutions. For the past year the number had been somewhat below the average but the committee thought it would be unwise to rely upon a recerrence of that happy state of things and had therefore assumed that they would have to meet the , coat of providing for a normal number of children. Parental contributions were still small in proportion to the outlay but in view of the promise of Councillor Ellison that the matter should receive attention he would say nothing further. On the School Manage- ment Committee the total deficiency was estimated at £1,212 us. sd. Taking the Higher Education Depart- ment of the School Management Committee, they would notice that the salaries in connection with the Secondary Schools showed an increase. The salaries for the Technical Institute, however, showed a decrease, and taking the two together for tile purpose of fair comparison, the increase was only 26 on the year. The changes were due to a re-arrangement of the work and the staff, by which they had been able to secure highly qualified teachers for special subjects in both depart- ments, and he thought the work both in respect of the day and evening classes had been materially improved with practically no additional expense. ((lear, hear.) There was a slight increase in respect of the Evening Continuation Schools, the figures being £571 Is. as against £536 125., but everyone who had the true welfare of the rising generation at heart must wish that the expenditure under that head was far larger. (Hear, hear.) The School Management Committee's efforts, however, to develop this work had not met with such success as be thought they deserved, and the mem- bers of the committee did not feel justified in inereasing the expenditure unless there was a reason- able prospect of an adequate return. The only other items calling for comment were those for scholarships and for the Grammar School. The item for scholar- ships was reduced by £5O and the possible grant to the Grammar School was increased from £3OO to £4OO. That was simply providing for the carrying out of the offer which was made by the committee and approved by the Council, that on certain conditions any deficiency in their annual income would be made up by the committee to the extent of £4OO. In return the governors of the Grammar School were to comply with certain conditions one of which was the granting of scholarships. The Grammar School had expressed their willingness to meet those conditions, and the estimates for scholar- ships and the proposed grant therefore appeared as be had stated. On the receipt side for higher education they would notice an estimated decrease on "customs and excise " from £973 9s. Id. to £OOO. Whether owing otherrteo e the et pya eeu rasser uss a the deci oed ul ont grointbeukntnci know of o temperancewnsbfurtoin from we ausscustoms ao mfact oratorsndt h eao xter istone ttho e rl oschaalr e taxation tiohne shoe d a lblee de n dwi mh isk vsh s a minog,e and so expected to be less. (Laughter and hear, hr). The 'Nies slight change in the estimated grants for the Secondary school (estimated decrease £750 to £700) was due to code changes. The grants for pupil teachers they esti- mated at LW as against £2OO for the year now ending. That amount was the maximum so far as the present regulations permitted, but there was occasion for hope, as it was announced that day that Mr. Birrell had declared that further grants would be made in this direc- tion. (Hear, hear.) He was sure they would all welcome that announcement. Of course they knew nothing definite, but it was scarcely probable, however, that the additional allowances under the heads of secondary education and the training of teachers now promised would be of such an amount as to materially affect their income. The capitation grants from the counties of Lancashire and Cheshire they estimated at £125, an increase of £75, Those grants were, of course, certain payments made by the two authorities in respect of pupil teachers and technical students attending the municipal centre and the Institute who resided outside the county borough. The item of £55 for the sale of books and materials at the Institute which was in the closing year's estimates went out altogether, as the committee thought the pur- chases should be made through local booksellers and not from the officers of the committee. The net deficiency for " education other than elementary "was £1,300 13s. as against £1,292 Is. and he thought con- sidering the work which had been done the slightly Increased cost called for no adverse comment, but, if any comment were offered, it should be favourable. (Hear, hear.) Mr. ROBERTS: Why are the Technical Institute fees estimated at £lO less? .

_ . Mr. Jor.m.w, in reply, said that in that matter the Institute and the evening classes should be taken together as there was some rearrangement. They had established a standard of entrance examination for the Institute, and that was likely to reduce the numbers, because someanpils who were not quite ready to take advantage or the Institute would be referred to the evening schools. Continuing,be said that with regard to ELEMENTARY EDUCATION the item which had caused the most heart searching was the estimated increase of £1,400 in teachers' salaries, which amounted to £27,222 19s. Bd. as against £25,779 17s. 3d. The MAYOR : When can we expect these increases to cease? Are they to go on for ever? Mr. JOLLEY : No. Continuing, he said of the total increase £1,035 was due to the scale increases and the remainder to plovision for existing vacancies and for two months' salaries for the ' " Beamont "

School, which would probably be opened and in use during the coming financial year. There was an item of £4OO for stationery and apparatus for the new school. j This would not be a recurring item, but it was an initial I expenditure which could not be defrayed from the loan. j The amounts put down for drill (£100) and for scholars' 1 attendance at the bates (£5O) were as agreed upon by the various committee concerned. On the receipt side for elementary education the estimated grants did not , show any material change, simply because the existing ischools were full and could not, therefore, earn more under present conditions as they were earning nearly all that it was possible for them to earn. No grant for the " Beamont " School would be receivable during the coming financial year. The item for fees was slightly reduced. From time to time attention had been called to minor changes in excess fees, and the change in the estimate was to meet this change of conditions. The net increase in the deficiency in rf4ard to elementary education from £1,720 Gs. 3d. to £3,335 2s. Id. was more than explained by the increases of salary he had referred to and expenditure in connection with the "Beamont " school. The whole question of the staffing of the elementary schools had been a subject of repeated and anxious con- sideration by the members of the School Management Committee, and a sub-committee consisting of Canon Stevenson, Father Wilson, Mr. Upson and himself had been appointed and had already devoted much time and thought to the question. Without desiring to say anything which might, in any way, prejudice the issue, he must remind the members that they in Warrington had still a great deal of leeway to make up, and that though great improvements had been made the staffs of the elementary schools ,were still very far from ideal. (The Mayor : The pay isn't). He could also assure them that the School Management Committee fully realised the importance of securing not only that every teacher should receive a fair day's pay for a fair day's work, but also that every teacher , should give his orher best for the welfare of the chil- dren, and put in a fair day's work for a fair day's pay. (Hear, hear). Speaking in general terms, they got admirable service from the members of the various staffs, but those who had looked at the resolution of the committee, asking for half-yearly reports by head teachers as to the conduct and work of assistant teachers, &c., would recognize in it another evidence of their determination to do all in its power to secure that the local authority got a full 20s. worth of work for every pound paid in wages, (Hear, hear). He did not want to minimise the burden which the ratepayers had to bear in respect to elementary education and he did not want anyone to get it into their heads that they were rfgardless of that burden as they were fully determined to see that they got value for their outlay. (Hear' hear.) As to the Finance Committee's estimates thlsre was an increase in respect of water owing to a change in the system, but that did not affect the rates. They had put down £125 for water, whereas it had previously beenlincluded in other items. Strictly speaking, however, the item was a matter of book-keep- ing. The item for window-cleaning had gone up from £9O to £135. The work was done by contract, and the lowest tender had been accepted. In the present esti- mates £136 was put down for rates and taxes as against £6O, as the amount was previously under-estimated. The amount for gas remained the same, but there had been a little saving on electricity by strict care. The amount for interest and instalments on loans for the Bolton and Beamont schools (land and buildings) was .£1,089 ss. 9d., as against £3OO previously, though of course the reason for that needed no explanation. On the receipt side of the Finance Committee's estimate there was an increase of £so,the receipts for the current year having been higher than anticipated. Of the increase in the estimated deficiency from £7,243 18s. to £8,519 12s. 7d., £2OO was not a real increase, being a book-keeping transfer in respect of water and rates, and the remainder was on account of new schools. The esti- mates cf the Sites and Buildings Committee showed a reduction from £470 to £335, and were quite as low as was consistent with safety. Personally, he thought they were rather too low, and the members would probably quarrel if there was a deficit. The MAYOR: The members had a fit of economy and I hope others will have the same. Mr. Jonas": But there is a difference between paper economy and real economy. (Laughter and hear, hear.) Continuing hisstaternent, Mr. JOLLEY said the total e.-timated deficiency as shown by the summary was £14,732 195. 4d., an increase of £.2,839 12s. over that for the current year ; an increase which had been received with a considerable amount of adverse com- ment. Might he point out that this statement of estimated expenditure had been honestly and carefully prepared and frankly set before the community their financial responsibilities in respect of education. Might ho also state that great care had been exercised in every department of their administration to ensure that no money should be spent unnecessarily and that for every penny laid out good value should be received. As evidence of the thoroughness and efficiency of the administration it might be pointed out that of the county boroughs of Lancashire and Cheshire only one had a higher percentage of attendance in its elementary schools, whilst their cost per head for elementary edu- cation was the lowest in the two counties. (Hear,hear.) For their work both generally and in the preparation of the estimates he wished to specially thank the Director of Education and the Borough Treasurer, as the com- mittee and community as a whole were deeply indebted to them. (Hear, hear.) There was another matter which, during the last few days, had been several times mentioned to him by members of the Town Council and others, namely, the power of the Board of I Education and of the Local Government Board Ito interfere with the action of the local authority and to insist on certain conditions. For three or four years past a considerable section of he conam-inity had clamoured for the intervention of the Local Government Board in matters with which that powerful department had really nothing to do. Personally he did not fear any action on the part of the Local Government Board. Having, in other depart- ments of public life, had much to do with the Local Government Board he had no illwill towards it. He knew that its action was usually governed by a wise adherence to sound social principles, and he knew also the great and beneficial results that it had helped to achieve by coercing, when necessary, backward local authorities. Much the same applied to the Board of I Education and their determination to insist that in all new schools there should be plenty of light and air, , and that the environment of the children should be bright, pleasant and absolutely sanitary would not be challengedeeeie duplifting. n yeoune wh o had Theany central realeet r al national e sir e for lwell-being round, ysecuire buildingedacer dte taienstainnidsatrrdatieoef eerfnieeng, cyeaeldl d, both teaching, whilst encouraging local authorities to experi- ment and advance beyond the minimum national standard. I Though Mr. Birrell had not given the public as yet any details of his plans either for legislation oradmints- tration he had spoken with no uncertain voice as to his general policy. They might take it that there would be no reaction tolerated, but that the full powers of his department would be put forth to encourage, and if necessary to enforce better administration and educa- tion all round. It was quite possible that the Govern- ment might give higher grants for education, but if so, it was quite certain that they would couple with them stringent conditions. There was no prospect of the cost of education either locally or nationally, being reduced, and any education, such as he had heard hinted at several times lately, to ignore the regulations of the national authority—regulations wisely framed in , the interests both of the children and of the whole community--coald only end in defeat, disgrace and loss. , (Hear, hear.) Not quite one-third of the cost of our education was borne locally. He had no desise to deny that it was a real and a heavy burden, but it was part of , the price of progress and any attempts to kick against the regulations of the national authority would hinder progress and would be suicidal. If we, as a nation, were to maintain successfully the industrial and com- mercial struggle which was so acute and so relentless, we must owe our success in no small degree to better , education. If our British democracy was to show itself worthy to control the destinies of the Empire, and to do its share in lifting the subject races to higher levels of civilization, to continue to be a pioneer of human progress, it must be in a fuller, higher, truer sense than most people yet realized, an educated democracy. Towards the attainments of that lofty end they must each do their part, and theirs was to accept their financial burden and approve of the estimates as , cheerfully as polisible. The CHAIRMAN said be was sure they had all been interested in the admirable speech Mr. Jolley had , delivered.. (Hear, hear.)

Councillei SAN'ICEY 'seconded the passing of the estimates. Councillor SANKEY seconded. _ . _ _

The MANOIt asked when the scale of salaries for teachers would reach the maximum. The 3 were told at first that educational work was going to be carried on fora rate of sd. in the £. The committee, however, levied Sd ; last year the rate was Is. and this year it was to be 15.31. The salaries paid to the teachers exceeded

the grants earned by about £2,ooo.Either the committee were not getting value for their money or they had more teachers than they required. The ratepayers would have no objection to paying the ordinary managing expenses but the grants earned by the schools should be equal to the amounts of the salaries, or the efficiency should be so high as to justify the extra cost. He asked the question with regard to the maximum, so that the Council would know when these increases of 3d. in the £ were likely to cease, as the matter was becoming a serious one. It was questionable whether some small schools were not costing too much in salaries, consider- ing the grants they earned. Mr. H. T. FAIRCLOUGII said that, speaking on behalf of the Church of England school managers, he knew they felt they had incurred a large outlay since the Act came into force in complying with the requirements of the authority as to the provision of light and air. They were very much disappointed at the heavy expenditure attendant upon the work of the authority and they considered the committee had been extravagant. With reference to the schools with which ha was connected—the National Schools—there WM a considerable increase of salaries. If they were getting a better class of teaching at the school under the new conditions be could see where the utility of the increase came in, but he contended that the staff was not as good to-day as it was before the Act was passed. At that time they had only certificated men teachers whereas to-day they had about five female teachers and some were uncertificated. The quality of the teaching was not as good as it was under the old conditions and he would like some explanation on the point. Many of the Church of England managers were deeply disap- Pointed at the results of the committee's work as shown in the estimates.

Mr. MAY commented upon the clearness of Mr. dolley's statement and said that if they had had similar statements in preceding years a great deal of the criti- cism which he and others had made from time to time would have been disarmed. It was evident that there had been a strong and determined effort on the part of the committee to reduce the estim- ates. He would, however, point out that while there was no increase in the accommodation provided or in the grants and fees received, or in the efficiency of the teaching staff, they had been saddled with an enormous increase of expenditure which he found it impossible to estimate because they had no statement of the original cost of education such as was being given when the committee took over the work. The committee had increased the salaries paid but they had not provided any more accommodation for scholars and he thought that was a fatal mistake. The Americans when they had a dozen scholars could put up a shanty and give a first-rate education. The Warrington Education Committee kept the children waiting, and drove them into the streets and allowed them to be taught to swear before they learned their letters. They stultified the efforts of their attendance offices because they were too lethargieoF conceited or too strongly wedded to a policy of having. fine large schools, and therefore they would not provide accom- modation such as had been suggested by the School Attendance Committee in the form of rooms or iron buildings like they had in use at Stockton Heath. Mr. i May contended that there was room for economy n the cost of electric and gas lighting and said the head- masters should be empowered to impose fines for waste- fulness in this direction. The teachers, he thought, might also be responsible for the winding up of the clocks and the expenditure on stationery could be re- duced. Mr. May moved an amendment that the esti- mates be not approved but no seconder was forthcoming and he withdrew it. Mr. C. E. PARKER said the most important and serious item in the estimates was the increase of teachers' salaries and the practical point was whether or not they had fixed the scale too high. The test of that was whether they had a super-abundance of teacherl and whether they were keeping the teachers they had. During the past year forty-six teachers had removed to other towns beoause they had been able to obtain larger salaries and when the committee advertised vacancies they seldom had as many as six applicants. The fact was that there was a deficiency of teachers and the circum- stances he had just mentioned proved that the scale was not too high. It was natural for members to grumble and complain, but what the Finance Committee wanted was that the gentlemen who complained should suggest some practical way of reducing the estimates. The Rev. CANON STEVENSON said the question of salaries was obviously the one which weighed most heavily on the minds of gentlemen on the com- mittee and . probably of some outside that room. He would point out that the passing of the Education Act developed an altogether new state of thing in educa- tion, and the immediate effect of enabling the rates to be used for all schools was to at once create a great demand for teachers, which could got be met on the spur of the moment. It meant that more t-:achers would be attracted into the profession, and in time things would right themselves. At the present time and for the past two or three years the supply was very far from equalling the demand, and the natural result had occurred. There had been a corresponding increase in salaries, not only in Warrington but all over the country, and if the Committee had not paid their teachers at a higher scale than they had been paid in previous pears they would scarcely have had a teacher in their schools. As it was they had lost a great many through the scale not being very high. 'He submitted that the authority had had its hands forced and had been carried on to a large expenditure on teachers by a motive force which it had no power whatever to control. Having said that, as an honest man he was bound to add that personally he was very glad of it because he did not think that in the past the teachers had been paid well enough and he believed himself in the importance of the teacher. Considering the responsibility and the enormous importance of his work to the commonwealth and the efficiency that was required he thought that, so far from the teacher being over-paid, the contrary, if anything, was the case. The authority had been by no means extravagant and the increase of expenditure was due to pres- sure from outside causes. He was inclined to agree with the remarks of Mr. Fairclough. Personally the question of staffing the schools had concerned him very anxiously for some time. His belief was that the committee had not adopted a sufficiently good standard in staffing their schools—that was to say,they had had too much regard to economy rather than too little. They were now engaged in anxious deliberation on the subject. They started with the ideal that every teacher in the school&if possible should be fully qual- fled.. That was an laid which he for one held very dear and it would be a great regret to him if the committee had to go back upon it. He was, however, bound to say that with the increase in the rates and the whole expenditure, it would be their duty to consider whether it would not be advisable under the circumstances, and, he hoped., as a temporary measure only, to resort to a less qualified class of teachers, at all events in some of the smaller .schools. He would be sorry to see that done, but having regard to their duty to the ratepayers as well as their duty to education it might be right that they should adopt some such measure. The Rev. Father WILSON wished to dissociate himself in part from the statements made with regard to the want of increase of efficiency in the teaching staffs. With regard to the schools with which he was more intimately connected, he was glad to testify that there had been a very considerable increase in efficiency. ITS did not think it would be very rash to say that even if the staffing of the schools was not altogether ideal and what they might really desire, it was practically efficient and a cons:derable improvement on the staffing of the schools before the Act came into force. The cost worked out at .£2 ss. Der head of the average attendance—a figure which was considerably less than that which used to be paid under many school boards and which was paid by a number of other authorities. He believed the committee were getting gem value for the money spent in salaries. Mr. H. RCIBERTS said it seemed to him that if Mr. May's idea of the efficiency of the teachers resolved itself to some extent into their capacity for winding up clocks—(laughter)—his idea of the value of the teachers and the ideas of some members of the committee would differ very materially indeed. If a school master could not be better employed than in winding up the clocks he was not fit for his position. Mr. Roberts pointed out that Mr. May had produced no proof that there was waste- fulness in the use of 'gas and electricity and it was not fair of him to oriticise the estimates on such an appar- ently false basis. With regard to the increase of salaries that committee had sanctioned them a few weeks ago and the Council had confirmed them. Was it not a bit childish, having had their opportunity for criticizing and having taken it to the full, that they should come again now when they had committed themselves to the expenditure? The Maxon : I asked when it was going to cease. Mr. ROBERTS : I had not you particularly in my mind.

Mr. MA.v : I olid not say the salaries were too high. I am being misrepresented. Mr. ROBERTS contended that the objectors to the increases were acting in a very unbusinesslike way. Continuing, he said the operation of the Education Act had unquestionably lifted the market value of the teach- jog profession—and to his isind very justly. He did not know of any other profession where for over a genera- tion the members had been content, owing to the curious conditions which obtained, to give their lives to one of the most important and praiseworthy occupations, and abide by a payment which had not been in many cases even a living wage. He contended on that account that the committee were not paying more than the market value. They in Warrington had been, fortmn- ately or unfortunately—and it was a very moot point— free from the expenditure which had characterised the conditions in many towns. In other wor is, they had not had a School Board. If they had had one, they would have been broken in to .an Education rate of probably 2s. in the £ long since. Now, because largely owing to the fact that they had such a leeway to make up and had to levy a rate of Is. 3d—which was a very low rate compared with what obtained in most other places—it was such an unusual thing that they kicked at it with all the strength they had. Mr. Roberts em- phasised the growing importance of education and said the members of the committee would be worse than craven cowards if they allowed themselves to have their ideas dominated by any mere question of the pocket for the moment. The committee had had to provide new schools, and thank goodness there was a Board of Education which would drive them along if they were not sufficiently alive to what was demanded of them. They were not going to stop at two schools, he hoped.

.

They would have to have more. That being the case.all the committee could do was to see they got value for their money. Mr. UPSON asked whether the committee over-spent their estimates for last year. He noticed there was an expenditure of £7OO on the maintenance of children in indukrial schools and similar institutions and he would like to know the number of children in respect oflwhorn payments were being made. Mr. Jolley had pointed out that a little less than a third of the cost of education was raised locally. He (Mr. Upson)noticed thee in Man- chester and in the Lancashire County area the local ratepayers provided one half and he believed that was the general proportion in county boroughs. It was really very sad that after the authority had been working for three years, they should have to restate the arguments they thought had convinced members of the committee and the public generally long since. The charge had been made that the committee were not getting any better service for the expenditure than they had prior to the passing of the Act. Three schools out of twenty in Warrington were at that time properly staffed. Now they had a very fair staff in every one of the schools. Under the old system there were nearly 15,000 scholars in the schools but only 2,000 were being taught by thoroughly competent, qualified teachers. Now almost the whole of them

were being taught by thoroughly qualified teachers. Mr. Fairclough should look at the matter ,in the whole and not find one school I where local and incidental accidents might have reduced , the staff. If they divided the amount paid in salaries , by the number of teachers in the town, they would find it g.ve an average salary of £7B. Ho had been some- what interested in the outlining of his duties by Mr. May. The head teachers had a large number of things to do at present, and it would appear that the last thing they had to do was to teach. (Laughter.) He might say there were no electric lights in the elementary schools. Mr. May talked about head teachers having to wind the clocks and turn out the lights, and of giving them magisterial powers to fine people who wasted gas. He would suggest that possibly the teachers might also be asked to wipe the noses of the children, and black the boots of the members of the committee. (Laughter.) Alderman SMETHURST said the Education Com- mittee's minutes were put and confirmed with snch rapidity at the Council meeting that there as hardly time for anyone to object to the salary increases. He contended that the teachers were well treated before the Act came into force—as was evidenced by the long connection of many teachers with this town—and that efficiency equal to the present standard was obtained' Mr. Max then moved an amendment that a minute suggesting larger parental contributions to the:mainten- ' ance of !children in institutions for the blind and deaf and drunh be not approved.He considered the resolution uncalled for as it would seem to suggest that the Finance Committee had authority over the other sub- committees. PraxsroN said he partly promised to second but after Mr. Jolley's explanation he did not see the neces- sity for the amendment. The amendment fell to the ground. The CHAIRMAN said the committee were undoubtedly keenly sensitive of their duties to the town in the direction of giving the best possible education and endeavouring to keep the rates as low as they could consistently with reasonable and proper efficiency. Some of the criticisms levelled against tie committee were uncalled for The committee were trying to do their best for the b.:own under somewhat difficult circumstances.

Mr. UNSWORTPI seconded the passing of the minutes. Mr. UrsoN said it was with reluctance he took excep- tion to a minute of a committee of which he was a j member, but he.must draw attention to the following i resolution :—" That the managers of the public elemen- tary schools in the borough be required to obtain from the head teachers half-yearly reports upon the conduct and work of the assistant teachers in their departments, such reports to be sent to this committee after they have been considered by the managers with any com- ments in reference thereto that the latter may desire to make ; the reports to be submitted to ithe committee as soon as possible after the 30th June and the 31st December in each year. It seemed that while the object of the resolution was a good one, the means adopted to produce the desired result were not good, as it would add greatly to the clerical work of the head teacher, especially in a large school. Was it advisable to utilise the head of a school to do a junior clerk's work? It meant a large number of reports by the head teachers, whose time was largely taken up at present by clerical work. If ahead teacher was not able to prevent any shirking on the part of the members of his staff he would not make that teacher do his work by sending a report. It appeared to him to be a very cumbersome method of attaining a desired object. The teachers of Warrington fully realized that the increases were not necessarily automatic and knew that unless they did their work thoroughly they were I not entitled to the increase on the scale. The names of only two boys had been entered as prospective pupil teachers and if that continued the children would event- ually be taught by women. It was not that they did not get sufficient money that kept young men out of ! the profession but the policy of continual pin-pricks, 1 the irritation, whieh restriction brought upon the men, , which made them intensly dissatisfied. He moved as an amendment that the minute in question be not approved. Councillor Evaaas seconded. Mr. MAY supported the minutes. Canon STEVENSON. in reply, said the object of the resolution referred to by Mr. Upson was to have the a-surance that every teacher was doing his duty efficiently, and that the automatic increases of salary should not be voted unless there was frrmrance that that was the case. In answer to Mr. Plinston who drew attention to the appointment of Miss Barratt at the Secondary school, he said Miss Barratt was a new , teacher, and there was a difference between the Second- ary School and the primary school. The proposal was to nrevent teachers leaving in the middle of a term.

The minutes were passeti. STAFFING THE SCHODLS. The School Management Sub-committee having con- sidered the method of staffing the public elementary schools in the horonzh, resolved that a sub-committee, consisted of Mr. A. J. Jolley, Mr. J. Upson, the Rev. Neville Wilson and the Chairman of the committee (the Rev. Canon Stevenson), be appointed to consider the matter and report to the committee thereon. SCHOOL ALTERATIONS.

The Sub-committee--oltle—Sa-o-o-l-At-t-endance Com- mittee reported that they had visited the infants' department of the Trinity National School, and had requested the Director and the Surveyor to report as to the alterations which would be necessary to the exist- ing building so that it would meet with the approval of the Board of Education, and submitted a detailed report from the Surveyor as to the alterations necessary. It was resolved that the particulars furnished by the Surveyor be supplied to the managers of the Trinity National School, and that they be asked whether they would be prepared to carry out the alterations and repairs set forth by the Surveyor. It was further resolved that the sub-committee be requested to :seek an interview with Messrs. J. Crosfleld and Sons,Limited the owners of the land adjoining the school, with a view to ascertaining whetherany land cr uld be obtained for the purpose of enlarging the playground of the school. Councillor ELLISON moved and Mr. WALK En seconded the confirmation of the minutes.

Mr. UPSON asked for an explanation of the minute. Had the land been obtained, and at whose cost? Why had the sub-committee taken the procedure ? The CaIi:ERMAN : It seemed to be under the wrong committee.

Mr. PLINSTON was pleased Mr. Upson had raised the question. He had pointed out already that it was patting the matter in the hands of the sub-committee, who had no right to deal with it. Mr IVALKEn said the sub-committee were not anxious to take upon themselves the responsibilities of the Sites and Buildings Committee, but they thought they would be fully justified in making every inquiry before sug- gesting to that committee the desirability of building a school in the neighbourhood of St. Luke's. Mr. MAY : They are only seeking information and not interfering with the Sites and Buildings Committee. The sub-committee only wish to complete their state- ment and pass on the information to the Sites and Buildings Cammittee. Councillor ELLISON consented to withdraw the minute in question, and with that alteration the minutes of the School Attendance Committee were approved.

THE EXAMINER.

FEBRUARY 24th 1906.

The discussion that is pro- "SLAVERY WITH ceeding on the Address to

A TIME-LIMIT." the Throne has so far been rather dull, but really use- ful. It has brought out some more of thitruth about Chinese labour in Sauth Africa, and it has particularly servlr to display the attitude of certain ot the Conservative survivors of the late Parliament on that question. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN began it. We must do him the justice to say that he has never liked the importation of Chinese labour into the principal of our new South African Colonies. He perceived from the first that it would be bad electioneering business, and, to Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, what is not good electioneering is _ not politics. " The leader of the leader of the Tory party," as Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN calls him, has temporised, has failed to condemn where he felt that condemnation would have been the best and the most proper course, has, in fact, stood by his friends ; but he has never taken any share of responsibility for the enormous and almost criminal mistake made by his successor, Mr. BuitnErr-Courrs. The gentleman with the very large number of introdactory initials, is a Con- servative member of quite another type. He believes that whatever is done by the Randlords is 'right. If they want Chinese Labour they ouaht to have it. If they do not get it they will not be able to earn a living wage. Why, to have kept the Chinese from the Rand would actually have placed the Kaffirs in a position/to demand higher wages! There is a sort of erratic manliness in putting the case in this simple downright fashion. The view of the rnineowners is stated in its crudest form. The underlying assumption is that the Randlords must have cheap servile labour though the heavens fall. • Our own moral responsibility for reinstituting slavery under the British flag is not a matter to be considered. That is not the real question, says Mr. BURDETT-COUTTS. The real question is whether the Kaffirs are to be per- mitted to dictate terms. The conclusion to which we are led by such speeches as this is that there is no more horror of Slavery in the Conservative party than there is horror of Protection. The par, tv is in fact, unregenerate. What undoubtedly is horrible is to be found out—to be opposed by a Government and a party which places the real facts of the case before the country at large. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, who has had his own car- toonists at work for these many months past, complains grievously of certain cartoons. The MARQUIS OF SALISBURY, in the other House, tears a passion to tatters in denouncing the Government for something with which it had nothing to do,' and in striving to make it appear that there has been wholesale falsification of history in regard to this Chinese question. Lord SALISBURY appeared ,to be in earnest, in which case he must have failed to make himself familiar with matters with which as a member of the late Government he ought to have been intim- ' ately acquainted. We have always contended that the conditions under which the Chinese are now working in the Rand are the conditions of slavery, and we shall certainly not withdraw that opinion in consequence of such melodramatic exhibitions as that made by the Manoms of SALISBURY. That the Chinese are well sheltered, well fed, well

guarded, is entirely beside the question. The momentous fact is that they are not free men.

" Slavery with a " was the description applied by Major SEELY to the system which has been instituted in the Transvaal. Those who desire to be precise may adopt this rigid and excellent definitiqn. But the condition of the coolies on the Rand is slavery all the same,and will continue to be slavery until the time-limit expires and they are sent home again.

In a sense the Chief Con- THE CHIEF CONSTABLE stable, Mr. Luke Talbot, " ON TRIAL." has been " on trial "this ‘veek. Quite a sensation was caused in the town on Wednesday when it became known that, in the course of the hearing at Liverpool ASSiZ9S of the concealment of birth charge against Warrington women, Mr. Justice Grantham passed some severe strictures or. the Chief Constable for what he considered a faulty preparation of the case. The reports which sub- sequently appeared in the evening papers made it appear that Mr. Tslbot had indeed been guilty of a grave dereliction of duty, but within twenty- four hours the chief of the Warrington police force was completely vindicated not only in the minds of his townsmen, but by Mr. Justice Grantham himself in the Court wherein the condemnation was uttered. We need not, we think, detail the circumstances which led up to the incident —they are sufficiently intelligible in our report of the trial—but we are very pleased to be able to congratulate Mr. Talbot on emerging with such credit from a decidedly awkward contretemps. The Chief Constable has, during his eleven years' official life in Warrington, earned a valuable reputation for thoroughness, capacity and tact in all his dealings either with the judiciary or the poor people who so frequently make his acquaintance in undesirable circum- stanCes, and we are sure that the townspeople will share the gratification we feel that this " drama of misunderstandings " has had such a happy finale.

The Ray. J. F. Matthews RELIGIOUS on Wednesday evening Iwsrancriorr. made a very valuable con- tribution to the vexed controversy regarding religious instruction in the elementary schools of the country, and the mem- niers of the Young Free Churchmen's Auxiliary at any rate must feel that their convictions stand on a sounder basis as a result of the reverend gentleman's dispassionate and lucid exposition of the Nonconformist attitude. But the views Mr. Matthews expounded will appeal with equal force to a far larger community, and not only that, they will engage the serious atten- tion of educationists of other and all shades of theological belief. The question is one which demands the _widest and closest consideration, for the Government will very shortly have to define the exact nature of the instruction, secular or religious, which must be given in the schools. There is a demand for almost as many varieties of instruction as there are religi- ous sects, and the situation is complicated by the uncompromising attitude of bodies like the Roman Catholics who insist on the teaching of their particular beliefs during schools hours. We had thought at one time, that a basis of nude- nominationsl or Bible teaching such as would enable the child to distinguish between right and wrong might be agreed upon, but now that "node- nominationalism "is classed as a sectarian dogma, this solution of the difficulty appears hopeless. We are inclined to agree with Mr. Matthews that the only possible scheme is to secularise the schools entirely and leave religious teaching to the religious bodies to undertake in their own time. The work is unquestionably their's, and a proper realization of the fact might perhaps result in the quickening of spiritual effort which is so loudly called for in present-day Christianity. We shall be very pleased to open our columns to any elaboration or controversion of Mr. Matthews' arguments which our readers would like to indulge in.

The tale of broken noses STOCKTON HEATH and ripped umbrellas con- AND ITS TREES. tinues. Stockton Heath residents are finding a con- siderable difficulty in combining beauty and utility. They are agreed that the tree-planting will some day bear good fruit—not of the market- able variety—but they are also becoming pain- fully aware of the fact that many of the trees are a nuisance. In its excessive te3thetic zeal the Tree Planting Committee planted trees with a lavish hand, and the Parochial Committee is in conse- quence much exercised in mind as to the number it is called upon to move in order to make reason- able roOin on the footpaths for pedestrians. The matter gave rise to another lengthy discus- sion at the Runcorn Rural Council meeting on Tuesday, but the argument was almost wholly a personal one between Messrs. Whitley and Hallows two gentlemen who were never known to agree with one another upon anything except to differ. Nevertheless, Mr. Whitley made it clear that the tree-planting had been somewhat overdone for the convenience and safety of those inhabitants who move about the district after nightfall, and the Council agreed with him to a considerable extent. In the future, the Surveyor will keep a closer eye on the estim- able doings of tree-planters. At the close of the discussion the Council administered a weighty reproof to a member of the Parochial Committee who had,in the fervour of his eloquence, forgotten the vast, rmy immeasurable, inferiority of a parochial committee to a rural council. We hope, he will, in future, speak in tones of whispering humbleness, and remember that Stockton Heath is not generally regarded as the "hub of the universe. "

There is much that is inter- STATE OF CRIME ()sting in the eleventh _

IN THE Bonouan. annual report of the Chief Constable (Mr. Luke Talbot) which has just been issued. The record of the state of crime in the borough atiords evidence of a welcome improvement. While the number of indictable offences committed— 97—is exactly the ' same as last year, the non-indictable offences show a decrease of 49, the total number (including apprehensions and summonses) being 1,172. Of these 1,025 were convicted as compared with 1,056 last year. With regard to the indictable crimes 81 persons were convicted summarily and 6 were com- mitted for trial, the corresponding figures for last year being 84 and 1 respectively. Mr. Talbot's report gives us some insight into the manifold duties which the police are now called upon to undertake, and it is gratifying to know that the general conduct of the force has given entire satis- faction. No fewer than 13 men have served in the force over twenty years. Another fact which is worthy of note is that the gross cost of the Police in 1905 has amounted to £6,626 16s. 4,1. as com- pared with £6,686 2s. sd. in the preceding year—a slight decrease. Dealing with the various departments of police work, Mr. Talbot points out that 127 children have been licented for street trad- ing during the past twelve months and four licences were revoked for the persistent misconduct of the holders. On the whole, he considers, the Statute and bye-laws relating to this matter have had a beneficial effect, especially as they more widely empower the police to investigate and deal with parental neglect or misconduct. The "men in blue " have rendered valuable service in watching temporarily unoccupied houses and it should be noted that this c xtra attention is willingly granted. The efficiency of the force has been highly com- mented upon by the Government Inspector and another pleasing feature is the very flourishing state of the superannuation fund.

The Education Committee THE COST OF had practically no alterna- E DUCATION. tiVe but to sanction the

estimates submitted to them at the meeting on Monday. To the greater part of the increased expenditure the members had previdnsly committed themselves and the demands of the Board of Education had made another considerable outlay imperative. All that could be done, therefore, was to express pious

'hopes that doe economy would he observed in the future and to commit the ratepavers to an education rate of 13.3 d. for the . ensiling year. Of course, the 2d. advance is msinly accounted for by the increases of teachers' salaries in accordance with the scale approved by the Com- mittee and the Town Council. With the paslince of the Education Act a great demand for teachers was created, and the market value rose aCcordingly. As compared with other towns Warrington's scale is stated to be not unduly high, and it ia certain that the expenditure under this head will continue to grow for some years. This is poor consolation to the long-snff-wing ratepayer, hut it is the inevit- able result of the altered conditions of education. The provision of new schools to meet the Board of Education's requirements has also saddled the town with considerable expanse. It is open to question whether the out- lay under this head is being kept within reasonable limits. When it has been stated by so high an authority as the Chairman of the Society of Architects in Manchester that snitable scho-As can be hillt at a cost of from £7 104. to £lO per head in that district, it is somewhat sur- prising that Warrington's latest school as at present designed would involve an expenditure of £l5 17s. per head. The Mayor, however, points out that economies can be effected by dispensing with certain elaborations, and while these will oolv reduce the estimate to a cornoarativv)ly slight extent, we hope the committee will in future undertakings of the kind succeed in obtainingi utility and AfEnieney at i lower figure. Tf t s possibl? in Manchester or Salford it shonld not be Impossible in Warrington.

Allen—Rogers. A wedding which aroused considerable local interest was solemnized at St. Panl's Church on Tuesday, the contracting parties being Nfr. Thos. Bertram Allen, of Sheffield, and formerly of Warrington. and Miss Bessie Rose Rogers, eldest daughter of Mr. Wm. Rogers, pro- prietor of the Feathers Hotel, Bridge-street, and a former member of the Town Council. A large congre- gation assembled to witness the ceremony, the officiat- ing clergy being the R3V. E. R. Tarbnck, vicar of St. George's, Altrincharn, and the Rev. W. Bracecamp, vicar of St. Paul's. The bride, who was given. away by her father was charmingly attired in a beaver-coloured cloth coat and skirt, and ivory silk blouse, trimmed with Brussels lace, and wore a cream hat, ornamented with pink roses and forget-me-nots. She carried a bouquet of spray pink carnations. Her bridesmaid was her sister, Miss Edith Pendry Rogers, who wore a beaver-coloured costume of chi &In corduroy , and a heliotrope hat, and carried a bouquet of spray pink carnations. Mr. J. W. Jhnes, of Liverpool, a cousin of the bride discharged the duties of best man. The wedding breakfast was served at the King's Cafe, in Bridge-street, among others present being, in addition to the bride and bridegroom, and Mr. and Mrs. Rogers (the father and mother of the bride), the Rev. Canon and Mrs. Willis, the Rev. E. H. and Mrs. Tarbnck, the Rev. W. and Mrs. Bracecarnp,Nfr. J. W.Jones. Miss Fenton (Bux- ton), Mrs. Hill (Grassendale), Mr. R. Allen and Miss Allen (brother and sister of the bridegroom), Messrs. Alfred, Harry and George Rogers (brothers of the bride), end the Misses Edith P. Rogers and Hilda Rogers (sisters). The newly-married couple left by train for London, where the honeymoon is being spent. Over a hundred presents were received, and these included from the bride to the bridegroom a gold scarf ring, and from the bridegroom to the bride a diamond ring.

Gandy—Whittle. The marriage was solemnized at St. Wilfrid's Church, Grappenhall, on Tuesday morning, of Misq Ethel Lyon Whittle, eldest daughter of Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, town clerk of Warrington, and Mr. Harry Gandy, eldest son of Mr. John Gandy, Church-street, Warrington. The wedding was a very quiet one, only the members of the respective families being present. The Rev. T. W. Thrher, curate-in-charge of St. Clement's Mission, officiated.

WARRINGTON.

Coming Events The third and concluding subscription concert for the season by the Warrington Musical Society, will be given in the Parr hail on Tuesday next. An excel- lent programme has been arranged, and will consist of miscellaneous items, including Stanfords " The Revenge," Brahm's Rhapsodie, for alto solo, male- voice chorus and orchestra, Tad) aikowsky's " Casse Noisette " Suite, Max Bruch's Violin Concerto in G Minor, part-songs, etc. Full particulars as to prices &c., will be found on page 1. A concert will be given in the Hall Can, Assembly Room, Stockton Heath, on Tuesday, in aid of the Poor Children's Summer Camp. Apart from the excellence of the object of the entertainment the programme is such as should complete its financial success. The programme at the popular penny concert at Bold-street to-night (Saturday) will be submitted by Miss Cruikshank, Miss M. Gandy:, Mr. Hughes, Mr. W. Howard and Hanley's Concertina Band. Personal. We regret to state that the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) is still very unwell. He has been granted leave of absence by the Mayor. Notice. Owing to the great pressure on our space this week we have been obliged to omit several items of local news, including the report of the Chamber of Com- merce annual meeting. J, R. WOOD, Piano Dealer and Tuner,Friara Gate, and 47, Old London-road., Stockton Heath.—Advt. Free Trade League.

*,ttee of the War-

The following is the text of the Free Trade resoln- tion which is to be moved from the Government tenches : That this House, recognizing that in the recent general election the people of the United Kingdom have demonstrated their unqualified fidelity to the Principle and practice of Free Trade, deems it right to record its determination to resist any proposals, ,hether by way of taxation upon foreign corn, or y the erection of a general tariff upon foreign goods, to create in this country a system of pro- tection.

* * The fighting spirit is still with us and following Upon flan Parliamentary election and a municipal struggle we are now to 'have a contest for the elective- auditorship on Thursday next. The retiring auditors are Messrs. Bolton andilradshaw, but there is a third candidate in the field in the person of Mr. E. England, who has had considerable experience in financial work and the auditing of public and private accounts. A. feature of Mr. England's proposals, is that he guarantees ifelected to hold an annual public meeting at'which he will give an account of his stewardship, explain the public funds, and generally answer ques- tions relating to the income and expenditure of the Borough. This idea has been followed with success in other towns—in Liverpool for instance—where Mr: Morrissey has been in the habit of taking the rate- payers into his confidence. * * *

After his arduous exertions during the election cam- paign in Warrington, Mr. A. H. Crosfield M.P., spent a brief holiday at Cannes. A paragraph in the "World" from a Cannes correspondent makes the following interesting reference to the Member for Warrington : "A new form of amusement has been inaugurated—namely, automobile runs to Thorenc, above Grasse, where, at the height of four thousand feet, adventurous " trippers "may enioy a couple of hours' skating and " skiing," returning to Cannes (if they don't get snowed op half-way!) in time for L linner. The deceased gentlemen had spent many years in India, where he was principal of the Police Training School at Vellore.
