GARLAND, 51 to 65, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. " Beautiful Warrington." The work of this society is malting progress and on Monday a meeting was held in the National Schools, Church-street, for the purpose of forming a com- mittee to further the movement in Howley Ward. The chair was occupied by Alderman Bennett. A committee consisting of 15 members was appointed, and they will elect officers for the ward.— committee for Whitecross Ward was formed on Wednesday night at a meeting held in the dining room at Messrs. Cros- fields' Works. Elective Auditors' Election. The polling for elective auditors for the Borough will, take place at the Gymnasium on Thursday next Three candidates are in the field, the two retiring auditors, Mr. Laurence Bolton and Mr. Edwin Brad- shaw, both seeking re-election. In, addition, Mr. Ebenezer England seeks the support of the electors, and an interesting fight is promised. Mr Bolton is proposed by Mr. A. Browne, and seconded by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton. Mr. Bradshaw is proposed by Mr. Wm. Owen, and seconded by Mr. J. Rymer Young, while Mr. England is proposed by the Rev. J. F. Matthews, and seconded by Mr. Wm. Hickman. Lecture by Mr. W. Madden We have pleasure in drawing attention to the announcement of the lecture in Parr Hall on Thursday next, on " Daniel O'Connell, and what he did for Civil and Religious Liberty," Ey Mr. W. Madden. We feel assured that a discourse upon the work of this, perhaps the greatest of Irishmen, will, from such a source, be more than interesting. Mr. Madden, like many of his distinguished countrymen, is gifted with rare oratorical powers, and should be eminently quali- fied to handle his subject with an eloquence that is none too common nowadays. The chair will be occu- pied by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, the member for the Borough, and the lecture will be interspersed with limelight vices. Appropriate music and songs will be given by several well-known artistes Up-to-date Fire Places.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge St., Warrington.—Advt. Latchford Wesleyan Church. The congregational tea meeting in connection with the above church took place on Thursday evening. A good number sat down to the tea' after which, songs, etc., were given by the Misses 1k Carriage, E. Barnes, L. Evans, and F. Lester, whose efforts won much applause. Miss Moston, of Lymm delighted all with her recitations, and Master McCollam caused much fun with his humorous songs. There was also a quaitette and a trio by a male voice party. The choir very creditably rendered two glees. Miss Hartley was the accompanist and the Rev. Wm. Mellor presided. Mr. John Wright, Sunday school secretary, in reading the report, said they were in very urgent need of a new Sunday school, the present accommodation being inadequate and as a consequence their work was handicapped. The Rev. William Mellor, Mr. John Ditchfield (superintendent), and Mr. J Maile also spoke,urging the cause of the new Sunday school. WEDDING PRESENTS.—Large selection of Useful - S.

* Old-age pensions were discussed in a very practical and symathetic manner in Parliament on Tuesday, the subject being introduced by Mr. G. W. Barnes, M.P., and supported by Mr. W. H. Lever, M.P.—an interest- ing and significant unity of action between Capital and Labour on a question which closely affects both. " H.W.M." in the " Daily News" says :—" Mr. IV. H. Lever, one of the largest employers of labour in the country endorsed Mr. Barnes' argument in a speech which not only approved its end, but endorsed its method—a graduated income tax. With simple warmth of language Mr. Lever declared that be was willing to pay the price of a pension scheme; in any case he besought the Government to try a smaller plan if they could not at once compass the largest possible scheme. The speech, quite unambitious in forrn,made a deep impression, as utterances from the heart are wont to do in the rather synical air of Westminster. Mr. Lever's face struck one as remarkable. It is rather American in type, firm set, clean-shaven, high- coloured, a good forehead, crowned with a rather tumbled mass of hair, altogether a distinct, interesting type."

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN DROWNING MYSTERY.

William Byfleld, captain of the Runcorn Grappling Corps. on Sunday recovered the body of a man having the appearance of a navvy, and about fifty years of age, from the Weaver Canal at Weston. On &tturday night the workman in charge of the Marsh Lock in the Ship Canal heard a splash and cries for help, but could find nothing, The deceased had told people in Western Point that he had been at one time employed in the making of the Ship Canal. Both his arms are tatooed.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
FEARED BIG LOSS TO RUNCORN.

A correspondent states that the Castner-Kellner Alkali Company, Ltd., have decided to remove their works from Runcorn to the Tyne.—The step is being taken after very careful consideration, the chief reason being the excellent supply of electricity to be obtained ata low cost on Tyneside. The site secured is at Walls- end, in close proximity to a large power station. Already the United Alkali Company have works on the Tyne. Registered in 1895, with an authorised capital of £450,000, the company occupies extensive premises, and should the statement prove true that the directors have resolved to remove to Tyneside the loss to Runcorn will be n very serious one. Sir William Mather, M.P., is the chairman of the directors.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
FALSE PRETENCES AT WIDNES.

At Widnes, on Monday, Patrick Burn, alias Thomas Kelly, 13, Kempston-street, Liverpool, was charged with obtaining money by false pretences from the Collins Green Colliery.—Prisoner pleaded guilty to writing a letter.—From the evidence it transpired that Burns wrote a letter to Mr. Southern at the colliery, representing himself to be a foreman at Messrs. Gossage's works, Widnes, and asking him for money because ne had praised their coal to his master, which would mean a continuance of the order. Mr. Southern telephoned to his Widnes agent, and found out that no such man as Burns worked there. When the prisoner went to the Post Office for an answer to his letter he was atrested. In answer to the charge, he concocted some story about another man working in conjunction with him, which he could not substantiate.—The Bench characterised the case as a very bad one, and they sent the prisoner to gaol for two months.

RUNCORN PETTY SESSIONS.

At Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, before Sir Richard Brooke Mary Savage, 10, Leinster-street, was charged with I:flying been drunk and disorderly on the 21st ult. P.C. Wynne said the defendant was drunk and rowing with her son and daughter in the street. He took her home. A fine of ss. was imposed.—Thomas William Eccles was tined Is. for a similar °thence. P.C. Cameron proved the case.—Agnes Langley was charged with having stolen a pair of trousers from the shop of Mr. S. Dodd,pawnbroker,Princess-street. The evidence was that the trousers were missed and after a search were found in a bundle which a Mrs. Kavanagh had pledged for defendant.—Defendant said the trousers were returned in mistake to her by the shop assistant and as she was short of money she cut off the private mark, sewed a piece in and sent them for pledge. She said her brother was in at the time she brought the bundle containing the trousers from the shop. Once before she got a waistcoat in the same way and imme- diately returned it. The case was adjourned so that the brother might be called.

WIDNES SLANDER ACTION.—MINISTER v. • P IJBLICAN. Harvey v. Caldwell.—ln this action at the Nisi Prins Court, Liverpool, on Saturday, before Mr. Justice Bray Alexander Harvey, Baptist minister, of Widnes, sought to recover damages for slander against Paul Caldwell, of the same town. The defendant denied that the words used bore the construction alleged, and he also denied that the words were defamatory, and there was also an allegation that the statements were true. Mr. Taylor, K.C., and Mr. G. C. Rees were counsel for the plaintiff, and Mr. Rigby Swift represented the defendant* Mr. Taylor, IT.C., in opening for the plaintiff, said the plaintiff was a strong and determined opponent of the brewing interest, and had been a teetotaler from an early age. On November 15th the Rev. Alexander Harvey, speaking on behalf of the candidature of Mr. Robert Brierley, said he stronly resented the trade which Mr. Caldwell represented. In the words of the newspaper report, "As a lifelong teetotaler he did not think it was in the interests of any town that its affairs should be in the hands of the liquor traffic." On the following night there was a meeting of Mr. Cald- well's supporters in the same building. Mr. Caldwell spoke and remarked, "At the previous night's meeting a gentleman had said he was a lifelong teetotaller. The speaker was in a position to say that it was not the case, as he had personally served him. He did not mind what men said against him, but he liked every man to stand up and be truthful." That was a charge of untruthful- ness and hypocrisy. His Lordship : There is not a word of reference, so far as I can see, to his character as a minister. No special damage is claimed. Mr. Taylor : No special damages. His Lordship : Well, these litte election plea- santries— Mr. Taylor ' • This goes much beyond that. This can- not be treated as a light matter. Continuing, he said that when his attention was called by the officers of his Churchlto the report of the 'alleged slander, Mr. Harvey wrote to the defendant asking him for a with- drawal or an assurance that the statement was not intended to apply to him. The reply, through the defendant's solicitors, was to the effect that Mr. Cald- well was sorry that the remark he had made use of had annoyed the plaintiff. The remark was true, and was simply made as a reply to the plaintiff's boast that he was a lifelong teetotaler. Defendant had no illwill in any way against the plaintiff. The Judge again intervened with the remark that there was no reference to the plaintiff's character as a minister. How could the statement affect him unless special damages were claimed? Mr. Taylor : It might affect him with his Church, and he might be dismissed. The Judge : They have no right to dismiss him for anything of the sort. A ,Juryman : Hear, hear. His Lordship said he must:hear the evidence, and Mr. Swift said he would reserve his cross-examination. , The plaintiff then gave evidence. He said that he ' lived formerly in St. Helens and Wigan, and had been minister of the Widnes Church since September, 1900.1 He had always been a teetotaler--he had never tasted. He had never taken any wine, beer, or spirits—not even as medicine. There was no truth in the statement that the defendant had served him witha glass of something. On the following Sunday the matter was generally talked about in the Church,and two of the deacons told him that they would expect him to vindicate his Oar- ' acter. The Church and not the deacons had the power of dismissal. John D. Walker, deacon of the Church, said that no church meeting had been held to consider the matter, because they had always believed him to he what he professed to be. His Lordship : And you still believe it ?—Certainly. ' Mr. Taylor said he could not carry the case further. His Lordship again remarked on the absence of evi- dence to show the words complained of referred to the plaintiff in his character as a minister, and after a brief argument with Mr. Taylor on the point instructed the jury to return a verdict for the defendant in the ground that there was no evidence in support of the plaintiff's case. Judgment for the defendant was then entered.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

EARLESTOWN MAN'S BANKRUPTCY.

ACTION AGAINST A. WARRINGTON SOLICITOR. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C., an action was commenced arising out of the bankruptcy of John Peplow, late licensee of the Ram's Head, Earlestown. The claim for £353 2s. 4d. was brought by the Official Receiver (Mr. J. Grant Gibson) against Mr. H. J. Longton, solicitor, of Earlestown and Warrington. Mr. Gordon Hewart, barrister, Manchester, appeared for the plaintiffs,and Mr. Cuthbert Smith, barrister, Liverpool, for the defendant. It was alleged that plaintiff received and paid the stun with notice of an act of bankruptcy ; that the payment was void and contrary to the law of bankruptcy • and, alternatively, that it was without the authority Of the bankrupt. There was an alternative claim agaillzt Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, add Co., it. Helens, for £251 3s. on similar lines and also that it was a prefer- ence.—The hearing was adjourned. NEWTON CONSERVATIVES AND THEIR

===== 0002601_19060224_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
DEFEAT.

At a meeting of the Newton Division Conservative Association on Saturday, at Newton-le-Willows, Lord Newton Presiding, Colonel It. Pilkington, after thank- ing his workers, stated that in spite of his defeat, the encouragement and sympathy he had received made the election the most memorable event in his life, and if again invited to contest the division he would, given health and strength, do so (loud applause).—Lord Newton, replying to a vote of thanks, pointed out that , Colonel Pilkington polled several hundred more votes than any of his predecessors, and he could not conceive a more thoroughly satisfactory candidate. The electors had, however, in a misguided moment, chosen their present representative in preference to Colonel Pilking- I ton. He declined to believe that the recent course of events represented a permanent expression of opinion. There were two clear reasons for the Conservatives losing the seat at Newton arid elsewhere. The first was that the late Government had been in office ten years— a period enough to unseat almost anybody. He had never concealed his opinion that there should have been an election long ago, but the results of the last election would convince future Governments of the folly of remaining in office after having ceased to possess the confidence of the country. The second reason was that to a great extent organised Labour had gone over to the otherside.

HIGH WAYS COMMITTEE.

The Clerk reported that the Highways Committee had considered the estimates for the coming year presented by the surveyors. The whole of the improvement had been referred to a sub-committee, and the estimates for ordinary repairs, had with certain reductions, been accepted. Messrs. Woodward, Harrison, Wright, Baxter, Pilling, Whitley, Linaker, Antwis and Jones had been appointed as an executive committee to the Highways Comnaittee. Mr. Woodward said the Highways Committee were not altogether satisfied with the way in which the estimates had been prepared by the surveyors. There seemed to be a groat similarity between the estimates for one year and the estimates for another year, so much so that in fact it looked as though—he was not saying it was so—the estimates amounted to nothing more than is copy of the estimates for the pre- vious year, with, of course, some little variations. The Committee very much questioned whether the surveyors had in view the exact spot upon which material was to be used, and it seemed to them that a general provision was made for material in each township without any definite or clear knowledge as to where the material was to be used. If that was so he thought he was expressing the opinion of every member of the Highway Committee when he:said.that viewed from that standpoint the estimates were not satisfactory: The sub-committee which had been appointed was intended to act as an Advisory Committee to the two surveyors throughout the next twelve months, and to watch what was going on. It was hoped by this means to save a valuable amount of material, and to minister to the interests of efficiency and good road making. Mr. C. E. Linaker said it, was proposed that the I Executive Committee should receive lengthy reports from the surveyors and should keep is strizt eye upon expenditure and endeavour to keep the roads in a good condition upon the lowest cost. The minutes of the committee were approved. WESTON' SEWERAGE WORKS. It was reported that the Weston Parochial Committee had approved the following report of a sub-committee on the application of the sewerage works contractor for spectal consideration on account of rock encountered in carrying out the contract. After a lengthy discussion the contractor was informed that the committee repudiate all legal liability under the contract for the excavation of rock but without prejudicee were prepared to recommend to the Parochial Com- mittee the payment of the sum of £250 for rock met with in the whole of the trenches now complete and to be completed under the sewerage scheme, including a deviation in a north-westerly direction from Cheshire's-1 lane subject to his undertaking to carry out all such supplementary sewers for house connections as the Council may require and the prices contained in the supplementary schedule of prices attached to the con- tract with an additional sum of ss. per cubic yard for solid rock as shall be certified as such by the Council's engineer arid also subject to no further claim for extras of any description being submitted beyond what is pro- vided for in the contract. The recommendation was adopted.

WARRINGTON MALE-VOICE CHOIR.

The Warrington Male-voice Choir gave a very success- fnl concert in Parr Hall on Safer-day evening. The Choir has for its trainer a specialist in the person of Mr. W. S. Nesbitt. the well-known conductor of the Manchester Orpheus Glee Snciety. Tt WI% quite a natural thing therefore that the co-nnerition of the more experienced choir should be sought in presenting choral portions of the programme, a splendid ensemble being thus secured for some numbere,while the sensrate singing of the two bodiee of vocalists served to disclose a standard by which the younger choir might gauge its progress. Comparisons being thus unavoidable it is pleasing to be able to say that the Warrington male- voice choir is surely winning its WA,' towards a position of honour amongst its nnmneers. Nor is it conceivable that it could long remain under the magnetic influence of Mr. Neßbitt's personality, or listen often to the singing of a contemporary choir which so largely embodies his ideas, without makine a substantial advance towards perfection. To hear the Manchester men deal with the intricacies of the nine-part chorus " The old soldier's dream " is at once a musical treat and an object lesson. Tn the finely-graded crescendo which forms the climax of this magnificent part- song the high-water mark of the evening's 'choral singing was undoubtedly reached, the audience being manifestly thrilled with a sympathetic echo of that emotion which, originating in the conductor's temperament and communicated to the vocalists under him, is always consnicenus in a successful performence of such n work. Many nnints of excellence in the Glee Society's rendering of Sehnhert's "Song of the snirits " were, we venture to think. nndiecernible by the audience in the absence of the words of the song in the printed programme, and the possibility of such obscurities detracting from the interest of public performances might well be borne in mind. Nevertheless in this number. as in others where the pianigfrinto was senreme, one could not hut admire the roundness, the softness. and the exquisite quality of the voices of highest ranee. nor could one fail to note the complete merging of all individual voices in a perfect whole of organ-like tone. A vociferous encore greeted the first-mentioned of these pieces, to which nemnnse was made with the singing of a very remronriste setting of Tennyann'e pathetic -verses "Break, break !" The humour of Mickenzie'e piece of musical drollery," A Franklyn's dr.117P," nas entered intered into be the Glee Society with infinite zest, the audience sharing the fun with evident enjoyment. And here again the audience's anproval was monopolised by the choir, it being impossible to regain qnietwle until eimilsr composition by Sir Frederick Bridge, "Old Tnrnin," had been essnyed. Of the separate work of the Werrington Choir the bracketed items "Evening" and "The Warrior's song" were the best. ST. JOHN'S WARD ELECTION.

LABOUR CANDIDATE'S SUCCESS.

James Hall (Labour) 6OO Henry Thornton (son-Trader)

Majority

After a short but brisk campaign the bye-election in St. John's Ward resulted on Monday in a victory for Mr. James Ball, the nominee of the Independent Labour Party. General interest in the event did not run very high, but the workers on both sides entered into the contest with corlsiderable enthusiasm. Both the Liberal and Conservitive parties stood aloof, and the issue was therefore a clear one between the Non- Traders and the Independent Laloour party. Mr. Ball was first in the field, but as his answer to the usual questions put to candidates by the Non-Trading Association was not considered satisfactory, it was decided to oppose him, and Mr. 11. Thornton, who has previously represented the ward, came forward. Meetings were held by each candidate, and a fair quantity of mural literature was circulated. While the Non-Traders contested the seat solely on the point of obtaining purity of adniinistration and checking extravagance, Mr. Ball strongly advocated construc- tive Socialistic measures. He believed, he stated, in low rates, hut not at the expense of efficiency and progress. Mr. Thornton's supporters contended that Mr. Ball's policy if carried to its logical conclusion, would mean the wiping out of all business men, tradesmen and shopkeepers, and the setting up of a huge universal trading concern at the expense of the rates, which, it was to be feared, would lead to more corruption in public life than existed at the present time, or ever did exist. "If we are to wait for purity until Mr. Ball's Socialistic dreams come to ' maturity," it was stated "we shall have to wait for it till Doomsday. As we cannot afford to wait till then, we submit a candidate of our own, and respectfully, and with confidence ask all thoughtful men and women vclters of St. John's Ward to vote for Thornton. municipal purity and low rates." On behalf of Mr. Ball an appeal was issued by Councillor Stott who denied that Mr. Ball was a revolutionist and took Mr. Thornton to task for not ddvocating reforms for the benefit of the working classes. The Labour men in the Council, he added, had always been on the side of purity and efficiency. During the greater part of the day polling proceeded very quietly bat in the evening there was Quite a rush of voters. Of' 1,717 entitled to exercise the franchise 1,117 recorded their votes. The polling station was at the old Club-room, John-street, where the three booths were in charge of the following officials : No. 1 : Pre- siding officer, Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins ; poll clerk, Mr. a Skelton. No. 2: Presiding officer, Mr. A. H. Clive ; noll clerk, Mr. J. McAdam. No. 3 : Presiding officer, Mr. A. Starkey ; poll clerk, Mr. G. W. R. Baines. COUNTING THE VOTES.

On the close of the poll the ballot boxes were con- veyed to the Town Hall and the counting took place in one of the committee rooms in the presence of the returning officer (Alderman Tinnion), the Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallaway)' the candidates and a num- ber of their supporters. At about 8.30 the result was declared ns above. Councillor BALL said he should not like to leave the room without thanking the returning officer for the courtesy and kindness with which he had conducted the election. They all knew that during elections they became excited, and at times said and did things they did not think of doing in cooler moments, but the action of the Returning Officer had tended to smooth away all differences. It was partly due to his kindness that they had been able to go through the contest with- out any bitterness oa either side, certainly without any on the Part of the two candidates. (Hear, hear.) Mr. TrrotrsrroN seconded the vote of thanks and said he thought there never was an election conducted with better feeling on the part of the candidates. The work had gone on very smoothly and if anything cropped up which threatened to create a difference the matter was comfortably arranged between them. He pitied a person who could not take a defeat with good cheer. (Hear, hear.)°The result was perfectly right to him, and perhaps he would take his defeat as cheerfully and as satisfactorily as any man in town. He had reached that age that he need not press into the Council Chamber, and that was what he said before he gave consent to be a candidate. Fie only consented at the last moment. He was quite satisfied that he had honourably lost, that Mr. Ball had won and that he deserved the position he had gained. (Hear, hear.) Alderman TINNION briefly returned thanks and ex- pressed his indebtedness to the Deputy-Town Clerk and the officials for their services in connection with the election. The campaign, he remarked, had been fought in a true English spirit. 'DECLARING THE RESULT. - - -

A crowd assembled to hear the result declared from the Town Hall steps, and the figures were received with cheers. Councillor TIMM said he looked upon his election as an indication that. the general body of the people desired not simply 4 negative but a constructive policy of reforra and progress in the affairs of the town. It was a sign of dissatisfaction with the slow progress that was taking place, a sign of dissatisfaction that the pro- grammes out forward by some of their politicians were not sufficiently intended to benefit the bulk of the people of the town. (Applause.) He trusted that whatever views he might hold he should try to press them forward in a courteous and proper manner as occasion might require, and that his services would be rendered on the iinderstanding not only that ha was a member of the Council for St. John's Ward, but the general interests I of the town would have his most- careful consideration. (Applause.) He trusted by experience and attention to duties to merit the confidence which had been reposed in him. (Applause.) He could join with Mr. Thornton in saving that they had endeavoured to conduct the campai en in a spirit of mutual toleration. (Applause). Mr. THORNTON said if he and his friends had lost they had lost honourably. They had certainly done their best. Mr. Bill had won and he was quite welcome to take the office. He had also won fairly, as far as (Mr. Thornton) could see, and he hoped he would be a valuable member of the Town Council. (A Voice : "We will send some more next November)." Laughter and applanse ) Before the crowd dispersed "Three cheers for Socialism "were given.

NON-TRADERS' MEETING.

Lively Scenes.

A meeting in support of the candidature of Mr. Henry Thornton was held in the Hamilton-street schools on Monday evening. Mr. :lames Dodds presided over a good attendance and was supported on the plat- form by Mr. Thornton, Alderman Roberts, Councillors Pemberton, Barton, Ashcroft, Skelland, Benson. and Dickenson, Messrs. S. Kenyon and A. Hulme. The CFTAIRMAN read the following telegram from Councillor Brough : " Very ill. Cannot come. Hope you will have successful meeting." That telegram, said Mr. Dodds, was sent to Councillor Lord who was one of the best workers they had on the Council. (Hear, hear.) He wanted to tell them that the contest was not one they had sought or desired. Thy were forced into it. They asked Nil-. Ball to reply to a question which had bean put to all candidates for the Council during the last four years and WAS on the same lines as the question which Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P. and Mr. Robert Pierpoint had answered in the affirmative. He (Mr. Dodds) thought the question was one to which Mr. Ball, as a representative of Labour, would at. any rate have given a courteous reply. He (Mr.. Dodds) had always supported the Labour party in the North of England. He supported Mr. Seddon and Coun- cillor Stott and he also gave a: seat on the Council —which he could have had for the ask ing —to Councillor Wright. He had shown his syrnpithy with Labour and he thought he was entitled to a courteous. reply from Mr. Ball, but he did not get one. He denied that Mr. Ball or any other workingman could find. anything against him during his thirty years dealing with work- ingmen at Milani forge. Mr. Ball represented what he might call the extreme wing of the Labour party. A Labour gentleman said to him (Mr. Dodds) only the previous day that" it was like the tail wanting to wag the dog." The great Labour party in Warrington was not connected with the independent Labour Party and it was the latter party that was trying to get hold of the lead of the whole Labour party for their own purposes and ends." He was not going to say a word against socialism. He had never during the three or four years' fight the Non Traders had had supported amen because he was a Liberal,Tory,Labour Man,Atheist, or Socialist neither had he opposed any candidate on that ground. He went against the Non Trading party by supporting Councillor Stott because he accepted the Non-Trading platform. If Mr. Ball had replied that he, was in favour of the principle laid down in the questions, and which seemed reasonable enough, there would have been no opposition to him. Speaking on the general question of trading. Mr. Dodds said that, as a result of the improper and immoral dealings between members of the Council and the Corporation the town had been saddled with reckless expenditure and an amount of extravagance which it would take years to rc over from. It was held to be quite right that these men should do the trade.because the officials could keep them straight. Hut how could they expe3t the officials to do that when members of the Council were supplying goods ? He (Mr. Dodds) said seriously that instil they got rid not only of some members of the Council still left,but also of some of the chief officials—

he was not blaming them because it was the result of the disgraceful system which had gone on for several years —they would never really get rid of the improper dealing between members of the Council and the Corporation and of the bad character which Warrington had in the face of the country. (Applause). Alderman ROBERTS moved a vote of confidence in Mr. Thornton as being a suitable candidate. There was no doubt that a great deal of extravagance had taken place and he was pleased that the non-trading party had stepped forward to put a check to it. me non-traders had, he believed, worked the agitation not for them- selves but in the interests of the whole of the ratepayers. So long as he found them going on the right "tack " he was with them. (Applause.) Councillor PEMBERTON said that had it not been for the education rate it would have been shown that the non-trading agitation had reduced the rat3s by is. Deal- ing with the waterworks question,Councillor Pemberton said that within twelve months the Council would be asked to spend another £40,000 on that account. He contended that an expenditure of £lOO,OOO would have been sufficient to meet the needs of Warrington for forty years, but instead of that the ratepayers had to find £250,000. They would in about twelve months have to commence to repay the principal and interest on the £150,000 which ought never to have been spent. Last year they lost £2,000 on the waterworks and when the next Corporation accounts came out it would be shown that there had been a loss for the past year of £3,800. Continuing, Councillor Pemberton contended that if the tramways and electricity depart- ments had been private concerns they would have made a profit. The Non-Traders, he said, wanted to 'amend matters which could be put right now and not wait for fifty years for the carrying out of schemes such as the Socialists advocated. The only hope for Warrington was to get more works to come to the town and that could only be done by keeping the rates down. (Applause.) Alderman PIERPOCNT said be was not a Non-Trader but the Non-Trading party had always had his heartiest , sympathies and the best support that he was able to I afford them. He had watched the actions of the Non- Traders in the Council and had been compelled to come to the conclusion that their object was to serve the best interests of the ratepayers. (Applause.) Councillor BARTON said he had always endeavoured to give his support on the side of righteousness and truth. Those who were in the Council found it a difficult matter to pursue the course which would give satisfaction to themselves and to those who were about them. Those present would have heard what a thrashing the Town Council had to submit to the other night from an outsider who, because members supported the party which tried to attain the highest efficiency at the lowest cost, branded them as cheese-parers and false economists. So long as he was in the Council he would support the party which did the best for the workingmen and obtained the best equivalent for the money expended. Mr. A. linfz4F, expressed regret that he had to stand on that platform to oppose a Socialist. He would point out, however, that Socialism was an ideal and it was not practical to-day. The Non-Traders wanted something practical in the town's affairs. Mr. Ball, however, would not answer "Yes" or "No" to a straight question and how, therefore, could they rely upon his support being given to the cause of municipal purity in the Council? There were many interruptions during Mr. Hulme's speech, and it was evident that a large number of Mr. Ball's supporters were present. Considerable disorder ensued whenever Mr. Hulme referred to Socialism and Mr. Ball's position... .

The CHAIRMAN said there would have been no oppo- sition to Mr. Ball on the part of the Non-Traders if he had answered the first question theyasked in the affirma- tive. A VOICE : Why did you resign from the Council if you wanted to promote purity? Mr. DODDS : Because the chief of the Traders put up in another ward and I resigned to oppose him. (Applause). When Mr. Henry Roberts ran away I asked to see Mr. Wright and he came to me at the office and said he was entirely with us. I then put bills on the walls stating that as Mr. Wright satisfied me I would not stand against him. (Applause). lam willing to do whatever I can for the cause but my friends think, and I think, I am better as a watch dog out of the Council, but ready to go in when called upon and to keep out men like Mr. Ball who wont answer a straightforward question. (Loud applause). The Kingdom of Heaven is first pure and then peaceable. I say there should be no peace in Warrington until you have purified the Council. The only way you can do that is by sending men like Mr. Thornton to the Council. (Applause and laughter). Several members of the audience still persisted in interrupting but eventually the resolution was put and the Chairman declared it carried by a large majority. In reply, Mr. THOANTON said hell would stand for what was straight, honest and true. He thought there was not a man in the town who would say that he had not done his share for the good of the working classes. Further there was no one in the town who had stuck to the working classes and helped them privately and publicly more than he had during the last 33 years. (Applause.) Councillor PEMBERTON, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Dodds, said the labouring classes had no better friend in Warrington than Mr. Dodds. (Applause.) Councillor ASHCROFT seconded, and said that a Labour man had told him it was a few Socialists and not the Labour party who were opposing Mr. Thornton. This statement evoked considerable opposition, and for a time there were lively scenes. The vote of thanks was heartily accorded. Councillor STOTT, who said he did not agree with the interruptions, took the Non-Traders to task for issuing a circular against Socialism. He said some of the state- ments contained in it were absolutely untrue. The CHAIRMAN said he had nothing to do with the drawing up of the circular, and if there was anything wrong with it he was very sorry.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 24, 1906.

LABOUR MEETING.

A meeting was held in support of Mr. Ball in St. Peter's schoolroom, John-street, on Monday. Mr. J. Podmore. jun., presided over a large gathering and was supported by Mr. Ball,Councillor Hart(Manchester) the Rev. J, F. Matthews and Mr. J. Hampson. The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS in speaking on behalf of Mr. Ball said he was a socialist long before the Labour Party came into existence and was a socialist because he believed their propaganda was the application of Christianity to the doings of men. He was perfectly in sympathy with the objects of the Non-Trading Association, as every man desiring pure govern- ment must be, and he believed that if they sent Mr. Ball to the Town Council they would be sending a man to represent them who would be a staunch sup- porter of purity in municipal life. A tremendous responsibility rested upon the shoulders of the electors of the country and he was pleased that they had recently shown that they were determined in the future I not to send the rich man or the landlord,but the worker to represent them in the great legislative assembly of the country. One reason why he supported Mr. Ball was that he believed with him that the education of the country—elethentary and secondary—should be abso- lutely free, and that it should be made possible for any child to pass to the university and even to the pro- fessional chair, the country to bear any expense to which the child might be put. (Applause.) A resolution in support of the candidature of Mr. Ball was unanimously approved.

A lI9USEWIFE'S WEAKNESS.

Palpitation, Back Pains, and Flatulence Cured by Bile Beans.

When Mr. Charles Peel, an engine-tenter at a Sowerby Bridge factory, moved out to the hamlet of Triangle, near Halifax, he was hoping that his wife, who had been too weak for housework owing to stomach trouble would benefit from the fresh country air. But it was Bile Beans, the popular family medicine, which had eventually to be resorted to, and Bile Beans not merely afforded relief but a complete and lasting cure. Interviewed at her home, 30 West-street, Triangle, Mrs. Sarah Ann Peel said : " For along timer suffered severely from a disordered stomach. Flatulence made my life a misery I had to be so careful what I ate that I dared take but very little food, and naturally I quickly lost health and strength. Besides suffering a great deal from pains in the back I was much depressed. Palpitation of the heart caused me much misery, and I could scarcely walk upstairs, so acute were the attacks. My condition was simply shocking, and I was quite unable to look after the housework. For five years I was in this con- dition. We removed to Triangle, hoping the change would benefit my health, but I got no better, though I was under the care of many doctors and spent pounds on medicine of all sorts. . .

"Some months ago I obtained a box of Bile Beans. They seemed to touch the root of my ailment, and the pains began to diminish. The wonderful improvement was maintained. My present healthy appearance and condition are entirely due to Bile Beans ; my strength has been fully refored, the pains have completely i vanished, and my stomach s thoroughly strong again. lean do my own housework once more and find a pleasure in doing it. A medicine of purely vegetable origin, Bile Beans attack not merely symptoms but the very foundation of disease. That is why Mrs. Peel is quite well to-day. Cheap substitutes would probably have made hr con- dition worse in the end. Bile Beans are sold in sealed boxes, at Is. lad. or 2s. 9d., of all chemists, or direct from the sole proprietors The Bile Bean Mfg. Co., 4 Red Cross Street, E.C.

REQUIREMENTS AT THE WORKHOUSE.

The Clerk was instructed to advertise for tenders for the supply of goods to the Workhouse. Mr. Morris said that at a meeting of the House Com- mittee the Master of the Workhouse said he had not been receiving English beef and mutton. The schedule of requirements had beau so altered that such a mistake would not be made in the future.

The Master reported that it had been decided to dis- continue giving fish dinners on the Friday of each week and to substitute bread and cheese and coffee.

PADGATE.

A YOUNG IDEALS.—The Padgate Young Men's Bible Class were again very fortunate in having the Rev. John Francis Matthews to address them on Sun- day afternoon last. He took for his subject "A young man's ideals." In his prefatory remarks he stated for nine years he had been continuously addressing Young Men's Bible Classes and owing to his many visits to the Padgate Class he felt he was probably in a danger of repeating himself. His subject was, however, dealt with in a most capable manner and received the most earnest attention. He contended that a man without an ideal was like a ship without a rudder, a garden without A flower or a sky without a sun. Every man, therefore, should have his ideal, and gener- ally speaking this ideal should be along the lines of a man's activities. He advised the young men present that whatever station in life they might be fulfllling or what- ever work they were doing to use their best endeavour to excel. "Keep your ideals as high as you like" he continued "so that they will always tend to help you forward and to lift you up." Mr. Matthews also briefly referred to the necssity of purity in municipal and national life and contended that every public man should be clean handed and clean lipped. " Men," he said," can only have clean hands by having clean hearts and men can only have clean hearts by having high ideals." At the conclusion of the address a rather interesting discussion took place in which several of the members took parts Mr. Matthews was called upon to answer some very important questions which he did in a clear and expre- sive style. Finally Mr. Matthews was accorded a hearty vote of thanks for his kindness in attending and delivering the address.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON CATHOLICS AND THE

EDUCATION QUESTION.

At a largely attended and enthusiastic meeting of Catholics held on Sunday in Warrington the following resolutions were passed unanimously I. We, the parents of the Catholic children attending St. Mary's R.C. School, Warrington recognising that the duty and responsibility of educating our children belongs to us and to us alone, deny the right of any power to take this duty out of our hands or even to help in the discharge of it, unless this be done in entire conformity with our conscientious convictions. 11. We the parents of the Catholic children attending St. Mary's R. C. School, Warrington who number over 900, regarding our Schools, built at the cost of enormous sacrifice, as a sacred trust to be defended and preserved intact, hereby pledge ourselves to re- sist to the utmost any legislation or action of the Government which takes away from Catholics the right to have for their children Catholic religious instruction during school hours, given by Catholic teachers only, in Catholic _schools under Catholic

management, or that would impose any but Catholic teachers on the regular staff of our schools. 111. Resolved that the foregoing resolutions he signed on our behalf by the chairman of the meeting, Mr. John Eckemley, and by the secretary, Mr. J. W. McAvoy; and that copies of the same be forwarded without delay to the Prime Minister, the Minister of Education, our own Member of Parliament, and the Press.

Secondary _School Concert.

There was a large audience at the Parr Hall on Friday evening when the scholars attending the Secondary school gave their first annual concert. The I event proved a gratifying success and as a result the funds of the School Athletic Club will benefit. The programme was an interesting one and although in sortie respects there was room for improvement, the performers on the whole acquitted themselves very creditably and were rewarded with the hearty applause of the audience. Miscellaneous items comprised the first part of the entertainment which was arranged by Mr. R. H. Al bery, Miss E. Frost, Miss M. L. Mitchell I and Mr. J. D. Baxter. There were several choruses by the secondary school choir and the school orchestra assisted by a nunatcr of friends, played selections in pleasing style. Mr. R. FL Alhety conducted. Special features which proved exceptionally popular were the eostume song "six little wives" "from San Toy" by E. J. Mee(Yeu How)and six girls who all took their parts well and the spirited selection"Rataplar"from the" Daughter of the Regiment " by Mr. Jolley and selected girls who appeared in bright, military costumes. Violet Brown sang " Vanika's song " with taste and expres- sion and was accorded an encore and later she was associated with Mary Hackett in the pretty duet " I know a bank." Lena Duckworth and Mitchell Price were responsible for an effective interpretation of "Boy and girl" from " The country girl." The most successful feature of the concert was the extravaganza " The Knave of Hearts "for which, it was evident, the pupils had been carefully trained. The various char- acters were admirably presented and the per- formance gave considerable pleasure. When all the children in their pretty costumes were grouped on the stage the scene was an extremely picturesque one. The leading roles were taken by the following :—King of Hearts, Jack Whitehouse ; Queen of Hearts, Lettice Warburton ; Ace of Hearts (Princess Royal), Eva Evans ' • Knave of Hearts (nephew to King), Fred Newbold ; King of Diamonds, Sarah Clarke; King of Spades, Lydia Pilling ; King of Clubs, Alice Shearer. This part of the programme had been arranged by Miss M. B. Morton, Miss L. M. Warburton, Mr. W. Donaghy, and Miss E. Wilkinson. From beginning to end the play, which is founded on the well-known nursery rhyme, was followed with interest, and the scholars were thoroughly deserving of the praise bestowed upon them.

130VRIL, LTD.

Presiding at the ninth annual general meeting of Bovril Limited, held at River Plate House, Finsbury Circus, E.C., the Chairman, Viscount Duncannon, C.W.O.' C. 8., said: It was a very great pleasure to meet the shareholders with a report of record sales in the home trade and of a larger profit on the year's business. The net profit was £152,530,being an increase of £5,970 on the previous year. (Applause.) If the accounts were passed the Company would be enabled to pay the six month's dividends on the 51 per cent. preference shares and 7 per cent, on the ordinary shares, and a year's dividend at 4 per cent. on the deferred shares, adding £20,000 to tht reserve (making it £165,000) and carrying forward £14,021. They still possess vimbos and its formulm, its bottles and labels and advertisements, and the ox in the tea cup poster. i They held them n reserve in case it was ever desirable to push a cheaper article than bovril. With regard to -Virol, Limited, the Board have realized that it would take some years of extensive advertising before Virol could become thoroughly known and appre- ciated by the public. They had therefore, formed a separate company in January 1900 for its development. The following was the progress of th• tVirol Company since its formation. The sales for the year ending March 1902 were 31 per cent increase upon those of 1901, those for 1903 were 35 per cent increase upon those of 1902 ; 1904, 50 per cent over 1903 ; last year 18 per cent over 1901, and for the last month of the present financial year 46 per cent increase upon same month of the previous financial year. —Mr. G. Lawson Johnston, in seconding the resolution, said that he had compared the stocks of raw material held by Food ipec ial is ts and by Bovril Ltd., at the end of December IWst, and found that it was not only a record stock, but was actually 46 per cent. larger than the combined stocks were twelve months previously. (Applause.)—The Chairman having replied to questions, the report and accounts were unanimously adopted.

DRESS-FASHION--HOME.

THE SPANISH MARRIAGE.

Doubtless the Spanish-English Royal marriage will bring to us something of Spanish character in dress. The Toreador hat we have had, and its first cousin, the turban, is with us now. Afantillas are very becoming, and may well come into favour, also high combs. Princess Ena will undoubtedly be a well-dowered bride. He. mother is a rich woman, how rich is not known, but Queen Victoria left much to her daughter. who gave up her life to her. Besides that, Prin- cess Henry was well off. Then, Princess Ena's godmother, the Empress Eugenie, is enchanted with the match, and she loves the girl warmly. The Empress is enormously wealthy, and so the future Queen of Spain will be suitably, if not splendidly, dowered even for so exalted a posi- tion.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
CABOCHON GEMS ON NECKBANDS.

Large cabochon gems are much employed on some of the latest neckwear, a favourite method of introducing them being to fix each stone into the three-sided spaces between the angles of the Greek key pattern which is so much in vogue, and which is frequently carried out in narrow Irish crochet insertion on boned linings of net or mousseline de soie. In this connection there is a decided fancy for big. unpolished turquoises, while, in accordance with the present craze for jet, enormous square slabs of polished jet, cut in facet's like a diamond, are inserted with effect into the centres of medallions of coarse lace or guipure.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
CHARMING GIRL'S FROCK.

Pictured in our illustration is a charming little " best " frock for a maiden of some fifteen or sixteen years of age. The maarial of which it is fashioned is a fine supple cashmere of a pretty shade of Wedgwood blue. The bodice is arranged in the fashionable crossover style, being gathered on the shoulders and swathed

round the figure in a particularly becoming way. The upper edges of the crossover are outlined by bands of velvet of a slightly darker shade. from beneath which springs a scarf-like drapery of pretty string-coloured lace. Similar lace forms the dainty chemisette and neckband, and edges the velvet cuffs of the three-quarter sleeve.s. The skirt is quite simple, the front breadth being plain and arranged as a panel, the remainder being slightly gathered round the hips to secure the necessary flare at the hem. A lino of machine-stitching decorates the top of the horn, and makes a pretty finish to the frock NEW MODES IN HATE.

The new modes in hats are making a modest appearance, now that the cheap sales are over. We shall certainly see bright-coloured headgear as the spring advances, declares a fashion writer in the Globe. Fancy shapes in horsehair are to be the rage, and from the one or two examples submitted to us there is nothing to fear from it, and much to welcome. Of course, bright- hued hats are rocks that people without taste split upon. However, there was never a fashion yet that could control that point. To us it seems, if our heads are to be decked with horsehair hats in rose. and green, and blue, and mauve, that either our gowns must match, or that they must oe neutral in tone. Mixtures in colour are seldom successful, we know possibly ten women who can accomplish them with a good, effect, and they are not British. The fact is, tho flair for combining strong colour cannot be acquired; mingling pale tones is an easier matter, and one which amateurs can manage quite well. Often it happens that two strong tones look all right together off, but don them, and they seem to conspire together to vulgarise their wearer. Lace will be largely used as hat trimming, and, of course, flowers. DRESSY AFTERNOON BLOUSE.

Our illustration shows a dressy afternoon blouse of mauve and white delaine. It is made with a deep yoke, which is brought down to the waist line in the centre, both back and front. It is eutliibid with a piping of mauve glace ribbon, and adorned with flat buttons covered with the same coloured -lace The neck is cut out

to a V, 'the small revers of white glace being also edged with mauve piping, a knot of the same coloured ribbon giving a pretty finish. The opening at the neck is filled in with a small front and collar of coarse lace, and the sleeves are drawn into long cuffs and finished at the wrists with little turned-back cuffs to match the revers.

The prizes are well worth trying for, and, as they are three in number, both for the boys and girls, you all stand a very good chance. ' Next month I shall announce another competition for boys( and girls; so be on the look-out for the varticulars, which will appear in this paper shortly. I am glad to see, from the many letters I have received, that you are all so pleased with the Children's Corner, and find the letters, contributions, etc., interesting. It certainly has made great progress, ,and we now have over i,500 members. Some of you have asked me for the date of my birthday, so that you can put it in your birthday- books. Weil, it is on June 26th, and I hope, now that you know the date, you will not forget to write me a nice letter on that day. 1 will give a very nice book to the one who sends me the correct solutions to the puzzles printed below. These puzzles were se* me by my niece Edith Kirk; so, of coarse, she may not compete, but I have sent her a very nice box of chocolates for sending me such a good set of puzzles. I must thank Leonard and Tom Oram for their letters, and the contribution I hope to publish later. Tom Oram's suggestion for making me a Present is very kind, but, I fear, not very Practicable.

With all good wishes, and hoping to have plenty of letters from you this week, Your affectionate UNCLE NED.

A CONCEITED ROSEBUD. (A Fable.) In a sunny corner of the garden grew a . rose-bush, and year by year, all the long • summer through, she bore the most beautiful roses; and she was very proud of her chil- dren, and joyful when each tiny bud had become a full-grown flower. The sun, the wind, and the rain rejoiced in their loveli- • ness, too, as well as the people who saw them. In the spring all the rosebuds dis- cussed the time wqn they would blow and see the world. But one of them held a different opinion. "I shall not bloom when my brothers and sisters do," he said to Mother Rose-bush, "for then I should not be noticed among so many." "My dear little bud," said his mother sadly, it will be better to bloom when your brothers do, for perhaps -you will have no chance later ()du- But the silly bud took no notice, and the summer his companions opened eir petals and formed a beautiful sight. "nen the last rose had g-one, the wind was not so kind as it had been, but the foolish rosebud was too excited to notice that. " Now," he cried, triumphantly, "to-morrow will be mine." He made haste to unfold the green sheath, but in the night " jack Frost" came by and crushed him in his cruel fingers. So, after all, the rosebud never bloomed at all for in the morning he was dead, and all his vain desire to out-do his companions.

A CONTRIBUTION FROM GLADYS M. KENDALL. MISS PUSSY'S PORRIDGE. Miss Pussy Cat did not like porridge She never could eat it, she said; She cried, and she grumbled, and wanted A mouse for her breakfast instead.

"No, no! " said her mother, "the porridge Will make you grow fat and so strong; A mouse you shall have when you've finished, Now gobble it up, don't be long.

"Eat spoonful by spoonful with patience: Each one is one less to the last; No duty is better for waiting: Begin, and the worst will be past."

Miss Puss) Cat ate up her porridge: It was not as bad as she thought; And now she will cat with a relish The mouse that her mother has caught.

THE BALL.

.. hat are you making, mother ? " "A ill for you, Arthur." " What will it be aade of? " "It will be made of cork, india- :ubber, Cotton, and worsted." "Where does cork come from, mother ? " "It comes from Spain." "What is cork, mother ? " "It is the bark of the cork-tree." " And what is indiarubber ? " "It is a kind of gum, which comes from a plant bearing the same name, i that grows n South America." "What is cotton, mother ? " "It is the produce of the cotton-tree, which grows in the West Indies." "And what is worsted?" "Worsted is made from wool, the fleece of sheep, which live in England, as you know, Arthur." "But, mother, sheep are not red, and blue, and yellow, and purple, like that worsted." "No, my dear, the wool has been dyed these various colours." "What shall I give you for making such a nice ball, mother ? " "You must give me a kiss, my dear,, and tell me from how many countries the different articles used in making this ball contains." "Let me see, I will count them. Cork from Spain (one), imliarubber from South America (two), cotton from the West Indies (three), and worsted, which we get in England (four), mother." "Yes, Arthur, that is right; and now your ball is ready." " Thank you, dear mother. Now let me give you a kiss."

A CONTRIBUTION FROM NANCY PORTER. - THE BRAVE FIREMAN.

Two little children were lying fast asleep in bed one winter night. Their alms were round about each other, and their dreams were of happy things. Suddenly a loud, crashing noise was heard—so loud that it woke both Nellie and Dolly at once. They clung to each other, and then they started up in bed and cried out, for the window was being broken in, and the .glass seemed to be flying to pieces, while a man was climbing into the room. The room was so full of smoke that they could hardly breathe. It made them cough, it got into their eyes, and they felt so hot. The moment the man got into the room, he ran to the bed and took the children into his arms, saying in a quick, gruff voice, "The house is on fire; come with me; don't be afraid." But they were afraid, more afraid than they had ever been in their lives, and poor little Dolly screamed aloud. The man looked so big and tall and black, and had such a strange sort of hat on his head! He went at once to the window, and looked out for a moment % Nelly saw from the window a ladder, which reached down to the ground, and she saw a great crowd of people below, and smoke and fire. Then she heard the Man say, "Not that way; it won't do." A great cry arose from the crowd in the street when they saw the fireman, with the children in his arms, i leave the window. Nelly won- dered what t was about. The smoke grew worse and worse, and the children could hardly see the door. Now it was. open, and they were out on the landing. But how were they to get down the stairs? / (To be Concluded.) OF NEW MEMBERS.

IMMENSE • PEAT SUPPLIES 2

Peat covers one-seventh of Ireland, sometimes reaching a depth of 50ft. The United Kingdom has 6,000,000 acres, of an average depth of 12ft. Germany's peat covers 11,000 square miles, and Sciandinaviata supply is thought to equal 3,000,000,000 tons of coal. LIFE'S DURATION IN EL:IIO.U.

Late statistics show that a Spaniard lives less than two-thirds as long as a Norwegian. The average duration of life is, in Norway, 50 years; England, 45; Belgium, 44; Switzerland, 44; France, 43; Austria, 39; Germany, 39; Italy, 59; Bavaria, 36; and Spain, 32. , A PLANET BEYOND NEPTUNE. The probable existence of a large planet be- yond Neptune was pointed out by Professor George Forbes a quarter of a century ago. His theory was based on the orbits of comets, but he has since noted perturbations of Neptune that seem likely in a few years to give a good idea of the position of this unknown body.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SUN'S CORONA.

The brightnes4 of the corona, or ring of light encircling the sun, was measured by M. Charles Fabry during the total eclipse. He found the light of a point a twentieth of a degree from the sun's edge to be equivalent to about 720 candle-power, or a little more than a fourth of the brightness of the full moon. The sky near the uneclipsed sun is 2,000 times as bright.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
DISTILLING GOLD.

Gold tittl*been easily distilled by M. Henri Moissan in the electric furnace, its boiling point being higher than that of copper but lower than that of lime. In a cold tube, the vapour con- densed partly in the form of moss gold, partly as microscopic crystals, the general properties being those of finely -divided gold. In alloys with copper and tin, the gold distils over last. On distilling an alloy of tin and gold, a purple of cassius is obtained in the dry way.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PHYSIOLOGICAL ZERO.

The physiological zero, or the temperature that seems to the human body neither warm nor cold, has been investigated by M. Maurel, a French physiologist. He immersed his body in baths of air and water of various temperatures, and has brought out the surprising fact that the unfelt temperature is considerably below that of the skin, or between 86deg. and 91deg.Fahr. A bath having our own temperature gives us a sen- sation of heat—a curious fact, due to the action of the heat-regulating mechanism of the body.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
A USEFUL LITTLE INSTRUMENT.

The "Association des Industriels do France" has awarded to a Chicago Company a prize of 600 fr. and honourable mention for a device known as the "Electroscope," which should prove valuable to linesmen and all workmen whose business it is to handle " live " wire or other conductors likely to be carrying high ten- sion current. The Electroscope, says the Maga- zine of Commerce,. is a simple instrument. It consists of a plain glass tube, mounted on a handle, and carrying inside of it a silver indi- cation leaf connected with its hermetically- sealed metal cap. When this cap is presented to a " dead " conductor from which the current has been cut off the leg lies flat in the tube. Should the conductor contain a dangerous poten- tial, the silver leaf will at once stand up. The degree of a potential of an unknown current may be approximately judged by the distance from it at which the leaf will indicate danger, since its action may be studied at any time on known currents. A blown fuse may be discovered by trying it on each side of a primary fuse-box. The instrument Li made in two sizes—the pocket size in a light aluminium case, and the station' size in a strong wooden one for use in the tool- box.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW ELECTRIC SIGNAL.

The fact that wires of all kinds passing unpro- tected through tunnels soon become (defective, owing to the atmospheric conditions to which they are subjected, says the Great Western Rail- way Magazine, often entails the making of special arrangements. For some years the wires passing through the Ledbury tunnel to a distant signal at the Hereford end „gave trouble, to obviate which an electric signal has been intro- ducecl. The' signal is controlled from the signal- box at Ledbury Station by a lever in the locking- frame, and when required to be lowered for the passage of a train the signalman reverses the lever in the usual manner. Instead, however, of pulling a wire, the operation has the effect of closing an electric circuit between the signal-box and the signal, in which there is at the signal a small electro-magnet, which is, thereby, ener- gised, causing it to attract an armature and set in motion a small motor, which, in turn, runs the arm to the "all right" position in about ten seconds. As soon as the arm is in this position a switch is automatically opened, cutting off the battery from the motor, but maintaining current through a second electro-magnet, the function of which is to hold a c:...tch securing the signal in the "all right" position. When the lever in the signal-box is returned to its normal position the electric circuits are opened, the catch holding off the signal-arm is released, and the arm then , returns to the " danger " position by gravitation. The motive-power for working the signal is ob- tained from a 15-volt primary battery, consisting of, about eighteen Edison-Lelanot cells, contained in the lower portions of a box formed by the base of the signal.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
"CONSERVATION OF MATTER."

_ The law whieh has been called "the sheet anchor of chemistry" is considered by Sir Oliver Lodge, the famous scientist, to be still an hypothesis—a "reasonable hypothesis" is the term he uses in his volume "Life and Matter." The law in question—that of the conservation of matter—means, observes Sir Oliver, that in any operation, mechanical, physical or chemical, to which matter can be subjected, its amount, as rneastired by weight, remains unchanged; BO that the only way to increase or diminish the weight of substance inside a given enclosure, or geometrically closed boundary, is to pass matter in or out through the walls. Such i. the law of which chemistry makes so much and which Sir Oliver finds far from self-evident. He writes:

"Its statemerit involves the finding -- of a -Pro- perty of matter which experimentally shall re. main unchanged, although nearly every other property is - modified. To superficial observation,

. . NOTHING IS EASIER THAN TO DESTROY MATTER: When liquid—when dew, for instance—Levapo- rates, it seems to disappear, and when a manu- script is burnt it is certainly destroyed; but it turns out that there is something which may be called the vapour of water or the 'matter' of the lettor, which still persists though it has taken rarer form and become unrecognisable. Ulti- mately, in order to express the persistence of the permanent abstraction called • matter' clearly, it is necessary to speak of the ultimate atoms of which it is composed and to say that though these may enter into various combinations, and thereby dis- play many- outward forms. yet that they them- selves are immutable and indestructible, constant in number and quality and form, not subject to any law of evolution ; in other words, totally unaffected by time,": At this point, observes /Sir Oliver, if we ask for the evidence on which this generalisation is founded we have to appeal to various delicate weighings conducted chiefly for practical pur- poses .by chemsits, and very few of them really directed to ascertain whether the law is true or not: •

EXPERIMENTS IN WEIGHING.

"A few such direct experiments are now, in- deed, being conducted with the hope of finding that the law is not completely true; in other words, with the hope of finding that the weight of a body does depend slightly on its state of aggbregation or on some other physical pro- perty. The question has even been raised whether the weight of a crystal is altogether in- dependent of • its aspect: the direction of its plane of cleavage with reference to the earth's radius; also whether the temperature of bodies has any influence on their weight; but on these points it may bo truly said that if any difference were discovered it would not be expressed by saying that the amount of matter was different, but simply that ' weight ' was not so funda- mental amid inalienable a property of matter as has been sometimes assumed; in which case it is clear that there must be a more fundamental -property to .which appeal can be made in favour of constancy or persistency or conserva-

===== 0002601_19060224_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
MEASURING INERTIA."

• • _ •

„. I Now the most fundamental property of matter known. we are told, is undoubtedly inertia. The law of conservation would there- fore come to mean that the inertia of matter is ; constant, no matter what changes it undergoes. 1 But inertia is not an easy property to measure-- very difficult to measure with great accuracy. It is iu practice nearly always inferred from weiaht: and "In terms of inertia.” Sir Oliver goes on to say. "the law ot conservation ot matter cannot be eoz:siclered really an experi- mental fact. It i. strictly speaking, a reason.. able hypothesis."

LETTERS' TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

_ Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

Lord Roberts and National Service. Sir,—As president of the National Service League, I am anxious to remove any misconceptions as to the attitude of that body and to place before the public a definite outline of its policy. The objects of the National Service League are. generally :-1. To ensure peace and security for the British Empire by organising our land forces in such a manner that we may not only be able to defend successfully any portion of the Empire against attack, but also that the strength of our defen- sive arrangements may render any attack improbable. 2. To improve the moral and physical condition of the nation, and thereby to increase its industrial efficiency. With a view to attaining these two objects, the League advocates that, subject to certain exemptions to be defined by law, including those necessary to pro- vide for the requirements of the navy and mercantile marine, every man of sound physique, without distinc- tion of class, shall be legally liable during certain years of his life to be called upon for service in the United Kingdom in case of emergency. In order to fit him for his duty he shall be legally obliged to undergo three or four months' military training when he arrives at the military age. This limited amount of training can, however, only be effective if as much military instruction as possible has been given to all boys previous to their reaching the military age. Such instructional training will, more- over, have a beneficial effect on the moral and physical .condition of the population at large. For this purpose the League recommends that the spirit of patriotism and duty towards their country should be instilled in all boys in all schools by the teachers. This is already done in some of our colonies, in the United States, and in several Continental countries. Universal physical training of a military character, aud instruction in the use of the rifle, should form part of the curriculum of all schools ; and in the case of boys who leave school before eighteen the continuation of this training' up to that age, in cadet corps, boys' brigades, and similar institutions, under State super- vision. The League recommends encouragement of rifle clubs, and the enl.eavour to make rifle shooting a national sport. With regard to the auxiliary forces, the League desires it to be clearly understood that its proposals, in the matter of universal training,are not intended to dis- courage the patriotic exertions of those who have given or are giving their voluntary services to the country. It fully recognises the self-sacrifice they have shown, and believes that they have done much towards teach- ing the nation a true sense of the duty that every man owes to his fatherland. While holding that the only real solution of the problem before us lies in the adoption of universal military training, the League will heartily co-operate in any steps which, consistently with this end, will increase the efficiency of theauxiliary forces. The League, therefore, advocates the adoption of all measures which will improve the military efficiency of the existing auxiliary forces and at the same time pave the way for their inclusion in a system of universal military service for home defence. With that object it urges the organisation of these forces in brigades, divisions, B:c., with the Accessary staff and equipment, as an indispensable first step towards their being able to take the field as a mobile force in the absence of the whole or the greater part of the reguhr army. I appeal to all who are in sympathy with the League's proposals to give it their practical support by enrolling themselves as members ; and I urge all existing mem- bers to renew their efforts to gain recruits and to form branches. At the same time I invite those who may not be in accord with the whole programme to give their assistance towards the early realisation of any of the proposed measures with which thevconcur.—Yours, Ito ERTS, F.M. Dace House, Victoria-street, S.W., February 16th.

ST. MARY'S DRAMATIC SOCIETY. Successful Performances in the Parr Hall.

The members of the St. Mary's Amateur Dramatic Society are to be congratulated upon having added another success to their highly creditable record. It speaks volumes for the enterprise and ability of these enthusiastic amateurs that they have given genuine pleasure to large audiences at the Parr Hall this week and at the same time rendered much needed help to one of our most worthy institutions—the District Nursing Association. The piece selected for production—" AB that glitters is not gold "—is a drama of the very orthodox type.lt is of more than usual interest however, as the whole theme turns on the inter-twined fortunes of people connected with the Lancashire cotton industry. Old Jasper Plum is a successful cotton spinner who has worked his way to a position of indepandence and is determined to cultivate "society " methods. His son Frederick is a ready convert to the new mode of life and his marriage to Lady Valeria Westendleigh gives unbounded joy to the proud parent. His brother Stephen—a typical Lancashire lad—will have none of the new-fangled notions and mortifies his father by announcing his intention of marrying Martha Gibbs, a factory hand. From these circumstances quite an exciting plot is evolved. A crafty villain in the person of Sir Arthur Lassell appears to mar the happiness of all concerned but, o‘course, his designs are thwarted in the end, the truth of the adage which forms the title is strikingly brought home, and the curtain is rung down on the usual joyous sequel. The homely character of:the drama appeals to many people and the creditable manner in which it has been presented has fully jnstifted the selection. The circle from which the Society can draw its talent is naturally somewhat restricted, but the organization has within its ranks some accomplished amateurs who have fully entered into the spirit of the play and have won golden opinions. The cast is as follows : Sir Arthur Lassell Sir George Collinge Mr. A. Wickham Jasper Plum Mr. S. Ryan Stephen Plum Frederick Plum Toby Twinkle Harris (Footman) .James (Footman) Lady Leatherbridge Lady Valeria Westendleigh Martha Gibbs Lady Mary Miss Ada Bradshaw This Hon. Lady Willett Several of the performers have figured with some success in previous productions and quite maintain their reputations. First honours undoubtedly go to Mr. C. J. Newton who is thoroughly at borne in the role of Stephen Plum, the plain, large-hearted Lanca- shire spinner. His conception of the part is wonder- fully good, and the naturalness of acting is indeed pleasing. As old Jasper Plum, Mr. S. Ryan, a favourite at these entertainments, scores another notable success. His assumption of the obsequiousness with which the retired manufacturer receives his distinguished guests is excellent, and he is equally impressive in the more serious vein of the father anxious that his son shall uphold the honour of " The Plums" by marrying well. Mr. W. F. Turner is well placed as Frederick Plum and fully rises to the occasion in the trying final scene. An unpopular part is that of Sir Arthur La.ssell, the cool calculating villain, and it is due to Mr. H. Burke to say that his portrayal is a well studied and satisfactory one. Mr. if. E. Murray is irre- sistibly mirth-provoking as Toby lwinkle and is accorded a full share of the audience's appre- ciation. The important role of Martha Gibbs is entrusted to Miss Delia Gleeson who fully realises its dramatic possibilities and infuses the necessary toucheS of pathos into her portrayal. Miss Cissie Sexton's impersonation of the winsome but weak-willed Lady Valeria is very -praiseworthy while Miss Annie Marron comports herself with fitting dignity as Lady Leitheridge and interprets the varying moods of that personage with fine effect. The various minor parts are creditably sustained. As a curtain-raiser the farce "The Boots at the Swart' is well presented and creates no little amusement. The parts are fulfilled by the following : Mr. Henry Higgins Mr. F. Eckersley Mr. Frank Friskly Peter Pippin Jacob Earwig (The Boots) Miss Cecelia Moonshire Miss Emily Trevor Miss May Kenny •Miss Sally Smith Miss Nellie Bradley Miss Betty Jenkins Miss Annie Stirrup Each evening an enjoyable selection of music is given Mr. R. Whittield's orchestra. The work "behind the scenes " is carefully superintended by Mr. A. Price, while Mr. S. Ryan discharges the duties of property manager. Theplays will nab!) he performed this (Friday) even- ing and the closing entertainment will take place on Saturday evening.
