TREE PLANTING AT STOCKTON HEATH.

MR. WHITLEY AND THE PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.

Lively Discussion.

The question of the planting of trees at Stockton Heath, Wit* again under discussion at the meeting of 1 the Runcorn Rural District Council held on Tuesday.At the previous meeting the question was considered and referred to the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee who ofter a long debate empowered the Council's surveyor to act .as adviser to the Tree Planting Committee and to say where trees should be planted. On Tuesday Mr. WHITLEY said it would be remem- bered that when the question was previously raised at the District Council meeting as no one seemed to be inclined to move a resolution he said he was prepared to propose that no trees be planted on footpaths in Stockton Heath which were of a less width than eight 1 feet. His action on that occasion had been criticised 1 in a most ungentlemanly and unchristianlike manner, not only by the members of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee but by other gentlemen whom the Committee saw fit to bring to their meeting to heap abuse upon his character.He was not going to deal with those gentlemen that afternoon but he would like to ask his colleague (Mr. Hallows) a few questions.At the meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial CommitteeMr.Hallows suggested that the newspaper reports of the discussion at the Council meeting only gave a small idea of the "wild and rambling statements" which were made. He added that he was astonished at some of them as various statements which were made were absolutely untrue. He would ask Mr. Hallows what were the rambling state- ments which he made and which of them were abso- lutely untrue? Not content with that Mr. Hallows must write a letter to one of the Warrington papers in which he said "I made no remark either for or against tree planting. I. only tried to correct some of the mis- statements and to have the matter referred to the Parochial Committee. This course was strongly objected to by one of the members who for some unaccountable reason wished them to deal with the matter in a most drastic manner on the information sup- plied by him without, consulting the Parochial Committee in any way. This on a motion by me and seconded by Mr. Linaker was objected to and the matter was referred to be dealt with in the proper quarter." He asked Mr. Hallows what were the objec- tions? What was objected to by him (Mr. Whitley) at the District Council meeting? Mr. Hallows said at the last District Council meeting "I think the matter should be referred to the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee who are the most concerned in the matter. I Complaints have been made about Mr. Whitley's shop where many-a-one has tumbled over obstructions placed on the footpath." (Mr. Pilling: "Hear, hear,' and laughter.) He asked Mr. Hallows to be kind enough to give him the names and addresses of persons who had tumbled over obstructions outside his shop. (Laughter.) With respect to the tree planting itself no member present could say he had ever voted or in any way influenced a member of the Council to vote against any- thing for the interests of Stockton Heath. Complaints had been made to him very frequently as a representa- tive of Stockton Heath about the manner in which the trees and especially the iron guards had been put upon very narrow footpaths. The police, the post- men.the lamplighters and the road-men had complained. The inhabitants generally had complained and he under- stood that both their Surveyor and the County Council Surveyor had remarked that the footpaths were entirely too narrow for trees to be placed upon them especially with the objectionable guards. In Warrington trees had not to be planted upon footpaths less than ten feet in width and in Stockton Heath he only asked for eight feet in width. When he suggested that he was asked if all trees on foothpaths of a less width should be removed. He replied " certainly not" but added that in most cases arrangements could be made with property owners and the Manchester Ship Canal Company for the trees to be placed outside the roadway altogether. Mr. Hallows said that apparently he and Mr. Whitley had two ways of watching the interests of Stockton Heath. He coatended that even then they had no right to discuss' the question at that meeting. They had a local Parliament at Stockton Heath, subservient to the District Council, and when that Local Parliament did anything wrong it was their place to call that body into question. Mr. Whitley had no right to bring the matter there at all but should have taken it before the Parochial Committee of Stockton Heath. _ _

Mr. \Woodward : Pardon me but you are entirely wrong in that. Mr. Hallows: I think the natter should be thrashed out by the Parochial Committee and then brought here. Mr. Whitley: Trees were being planted and if the I surveyor had only attended to his duty he might have prevented these trees from being planted. Since our last meeting scores of trees have been been planted upon narrow footpaths without authority from our surveyor. Mr. Woodward: Is that so Mr. Swinton? Mr. Swinton : Trees have been planted without my authority. Mr. Hallows said it we understood by the Tree Planting Committee that Mr. Swinton should not see e^ery tree planted but should exercise a general super- vision. That general supervision had been given. Mr. ‘Vhitley said trees had been planted on footpaths three feet wide. He asked that those footpaths should be mimed. Mr. Whitley : I said three feet only was left after the trees had been planted. . _

Mr. Hallows : Mr. Whitley also said money was got to plant trees in gardens and on the banks of the Ship Canal. That is not correct. Mr. Whitley asks for the names of persons who have tumbled over obstructions outside his shop. I have not tumbled over them, but I know people who have done, and will give him a few names if be will give me the names of the hundreds of people who have complained to him about the planting ,of the trees. Mr. Whitley also said that no trees had been altered. That was another " fib," because trees had been altered at that very time. He ought to he very careful before he makes statements to see that the statements are correct. Mr. J. H. Davies thought that the Council had done wrong in delegating its powers in respect to tree plant- ing to the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee. They refused to delegate similar powers to the Frodsham Corn mittee. Mr. Whitley rose to answer Mr. Hallows, but was ruled out of order by the Chairman. Mr. Woodward said he would like to make it clear that the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee had no right;whatever over highway matters. It was not the custOm of the Council to delegate its highway powers to any committee. They had refused to do so for Frodshom and he took it that every parish within the district %mild be dealt with on exactly similar lines. ' Stockton Heath must not expect to be treated any other way. Mr. Hallows : They do not. That being the caso it would not be proper to delegate to them the powers over the highway. Mr. Hallows : They recognize that and asked per- mission in a proper manner. The tree planting committee being only human had made mistakes and were already making certain alterations. It could not be expected that they would see everything at once but they were trying to meet everyone as far as it was possible. Mr. Turner : Who gave permission for the trees to be planted ? Mr. Woodward : Rarrnisliont was given here for the planting of trees in situations satisfactory to our 1 surveyor. Mr. Hallow : How is it that in Ellesmere-road, belonging to the County Council, where the footpaths are exactly the same width as thoSe in Fairfield-road, nothing has been said about the planting of the trees? Mr. Woodward: We are not administering County Council roads here. if the County Council make mis- takes it'is no reason why we should follow in their foot steps. Mr. Whitley said that the total area (including War- rington) for which subscriptions had been raised con- tained a population of 75,000, and yet Mr. Hallows was under the impression that the raising of £270 represen- ted that the majority of that population were in favour of Oka scheme. "Only last week," continued Mr. Whit- ley, " a gentleman hurried into our place of business and asked if I was in. He was informed that I was not, and said 'There is a gentleman who has just run against a tree guard and smashed his nose, and is bleeding there.' The following evening a lady came into my place and said her husband had torn A very valuable umbrella upon the spikes at the top of the guard." Mr. Hallows : We have not had a chance of rectifying these things. Mr. Linaker : Mr. Hollows has not exactly answered Mr. Whitley's questions, therefore I think we can leave the two gentlemen to fight it out alone, especially as both are capable of defending their respective corners. (Hear. hear and laughter.) What I want to say is this: I read the newsnaper report of the meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee with a considerable amount of interest and I must say I know of nothing which took place at this District Council meeting a fortnight ago which called for the remarks made by a certain Mr. 11. At the Liverpool Assizes,before Mr. Justice Grantham on Wednesday, Alicia Drury (35), of no occupation, and Beatrice Waterworth(37), needlewoman, were charged with having at Warrington, on or about October 19, 1904, concealed the birth of the latter prisoner's child by secretly disposing of it. Mr. Foard prosecuted, and Mr. Madden and Mr. Alfred Elias defended the prisoners who pleaded not guilty. The facts of the case have already been published in the "Examiner" in connection with the police court proceedings.

THE JUDGE INTERPOSES. After hearing evidence, the judge was supplied with a cony of the statement made by old Mrs. Drury to the Chief Constable of Warrington before the Police Court proceedings. An officer, on his lordship's direction, had been despatched to Warrington for the document. His Lordship referred to a statement in the deposi- tions of the mother-in-law taken before the magistrates to the effect : " Tarn sure she (theprisoner Drury) said she took it (the child) into the other room and strangled it," and compared it with the statement taken down by the Chief Constable. In this latter statement the mother-in-law said : " I think Alicia Drury said, •S‘ I strangled it,' but lam not sure." His lordship went on to quote some passages which had been interpolated by the Chief Constable in the statement as follows : "I am not sure of any of Alicia Drury's words being the exact words used by her, but they are as near as my memory serves me. I am sixty-one years of age. My son committed suicide on the 25th September, and I have not been the same in mind since, I have brooded over it night and day. I have been very depressed, and still am depressed." His lordship said he could not discover when these passages had been interpolated. They were written in the margin. He felt he must comment very strongly on the way the Chief Constable had done his work. He ought to have had proper paper on which to write out such a statement. It was most iniquitous, too, that the state- ment had been kept back. It ought to have been brought out before the magistrates as to what the mother-in-law's feeling was. Mr. Foard remarked that anything his lordship said would receive duo consideration. The Chief Constable was ill, and was unable -to attend the trial that day. His Lordship said that might be, but it was no excuse for what he had done. Mr. Madden said the prisoner Waterworth was in bed when the child was buried in the pantry. She had nothing to do with the disposition of the body,although she was told of the burial afterwards. The other prisoner. Drury, had been deceived by her husband, and refused to touch the child of shame. The whole pro- cess of burial was carried out by Alfred Drury. Counsel asked the jury to believe that the prisoners had suffered enough already in endeavouring to protect the man who had betrayed both of them, and to say that the case for the prosecution had not been made out. "A VERY SERIOUS MATTER." Reverting to the mother-in-law's statement taken by the Chief Constable, the judge said it was a very serious matter that it should not have been forthcoming at first. He had only succeeded in getting possession of it after a great deal of trouble. There was in that document information which would have thrown doubt on the case from the first, and it was quite probable that the magistrates never would have committed the prisoners if it had been brought to their attention. The words which he had laid stress on had been put in at the side of the document. He did not know when it was written—for the ink was different and the hand- writing was somewhat different. He was sorry to have w to say it, but he ne l know anything done soim- properly before.

VERDICT. The jury immediately returned a verdict of" Not guilty," and prisoners were discharged.

The Sequel. The Chief Constable Explains and the Judge Withdraws. At the Liverpool Assizes, on Thursday morning, Mr. Madden asked the permission of Mr. Justice Grantham to refer to the Warrington case which was before the Court on the previous day, in which his Lordship had censured the Chief Constable of Warrington (Mr. Talbot) for what he described as "improper conduct." He (counsel) had said in the course of his remarks to the jury that, from what he knew of the Chief Constable, he was a fair-minded man, and would treat justly those who were placed in his charge. Inquiries had only served to emphasise that opinion. A great deal of pain had been caused to the Chief Constable by the remarks of his lordship, and with the object of vindicating him- self he had attended the Court that day in order to give an explanation to his Lordship. When he had the state- mentsof old Mrs. Drury before him of the previous day his Lordship expressed the belief that the handwriting in the body of the document was different to the hand-, writing of the marginal note. That was quite right. On the occasion when the statement was being taken down the Chief Constable happened to turn from Mrs. Drury to his subordinates, and during the few moments that elapsed the woman took the document and herself r wrote the marginal note to the effect that she was not I sure that'Alicia Drury's words, as she had already stated them, were her exact words. His Lordship had con- sidered it iniquitous that this statement was not brought I prominently for ward at the Police Court. If it was not it could not be said to be the Chief Constable's fault. He had discharged his duty when he had taken the state- ment down, and as to the prosecution he had nothing further to do with it. The judge asked in whose possession the document was when he sent to Warrington for it.. Mr. Madden replied that it was In the possession of the Chief Constable, who would give an explanation. The Chief Constable had felt this matter very keenly, and had written to the solicitor who defended the prisoners in the police Court. In the spirit of fairness which the Chief Constable bad displayed he had shown this statement to the solicitor for the defence at the Police Court. He also showed it to the prosecution,but it was not put upon the depositions. The letter sent by the Chief Constable to Mr. Steel, the solicitor for the prisoners, was to the effect that he was gratified at the result of the assize trial, but he was astounded beyond measure at the remarks of the learned judge concerning himself. Prom the first he had great doubts as to the criminal responsibilities of the two I women, and he was against the committal. Knowing I Mrs. Drury the elder to be unreliable, he kept the rough draft of the statement by him. He had suggested that this statement should be attached to the deposi- tions, but he was over-ruled by the prosecuting solicitor and the magistrates' clerk. After twenty-seven years police service this was the first adverse comment the Chief Constable of Warrington had received from the bench. On the other hand, he had been complimented many times—on two occasions by Mr. J ustice Grantham, The Chief Constable (Mr. Luke Talbot) was called into the witness-box.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. [By " Ovalist."]

Batley again! Warrington seem fated to run up against Batley in cup-ties, and up-to-date their record against "the gallant youths" makes rather melancholy reading. Twice have they met Batley, once in the semi-final, and once in tho final, ;and on both occasions Batley won. Now the third time is at band, and let us hope that—like Warrington's third appearance in the final last April —it will pay for all. The Northern Union Cup draw has destined Warrington to go to Batley in the first reund,a visit which involves a struggle few teams care to face. Previously Warrington and Batley have met in cup-ties on neutral ground, but Warrington have won once at Batley in a league match while they have generally trounced them at Wilderspool. Oh ! for the shadow of the church spire once more !

Warrington should, however, send no trembling fifteen to Batley on March 3. The prevailing opinion in the town is that "the wirepullers " will win. Batley are far from being the team of old, while War- rington have advanced in recent seasons and never had so many competent players on their register as now. It is unfortunate that some of the cracks are injured, but a team should be available for the open- ing round which will be strong at all points. The " gallant youths " have only won five league matches all the season, against Warrington's 14 victories, so that the preponderating skill should be on Warring- ton's side. What they need to overcome is the Batley doggedness, and in the last few cup-tourneys they have shown themselves possessed of a considerable share of this quality themselves. Batley at home is unquestion- ably a" hard nut "but I, shall have faith in Warring- tons ability to crack it. ,

-- The draw for the first round of the Cup Competi- tion was made on Monday at Huddersfield and resulted in the following clubs being opposed, the first-named having choice of ground : Dewsbury v. Hull Kingston Rovers. Leeds v. Normanton. Featherstone Rovers v. Brookland Rovers. Pontefract v. Oldham. Halifax v. Hunslet. Swinton v. Leigh. Batley v. Warrington. Roch-dale Hornets v. Salford. Broughton Rangers v. Barrow. York v. Wigan. Egerton v. Chadderton or Leigh Shamrocks. Victoria Rangers v. Widnes. Brighouse Rangers v. Morecambe. Hull v. Runcorn. Egremont or Parton v. Keighley. Wakefield v. Bradford.

Lancashire has been rather severely dealt with by Dame Fortune, for Oldham, Warrington, Wigan, and Runcorn have all to go to Yorkshire, while not a single Yorkshire club has to play in Lancashire. In three other cases Lancashire clubs are opposed to one another. Still, the County Palatine may be relied upon to have a good showing in the next round.

It may interest some of my readers, and settle a few disputes, if I give particulars showing how far War- ringt6mhas progressed in the Cup Competition since it started, together with the names of the clubs they eventuAlly succumbed to. In 1897 they were beaten in the semi-final at Huddersfield by ,Batley 6-0; 1898, beaten at Wakefield in the first round, 5-3 ; 1809, beaten at Oldham in second round, 14-0 ; 1900, beaten at Rochdale in the third round, 3-0 ; 1901, beaten in the final at Leeds by Batley, 6-0 ; 1902, beaten at Warrington in second round by Bradford, 11-0 ; 1903 beaten at St. Helens, first round, by 3 goals to a try, 6-3 ; 1904, beaten in the final at Salford by Halifax, 8-3 ; 1905, won the Cup by beating Hull Kingston Rovers at Leeds, 6-0.

• Warrington's performance last Saturday 17'vas very disappointing. The team which went to Hull was by no means a poor one, and a defeat of 13 points to nil was not expected. The Rovers have thus beaten the Cup-holders twice since the final tie and doubtless feet well-pleased with themselves. At Warrington they were somewhat lucky, but on Saturday there was no doubting their superiority. The Warrington for- wards again played a very. robust game, but Clere was , only one team in it behind. The Rovers ran and passed splendidly and owed much of their success to the work of Dan Rees at centre, the international pro- ducing his best form. West was very mart on the wing, and obtained all three tries, Kenyon having little change of stopping him. Where Warrington's combination has gone to is a puzzle. The men operating at three- quarter ought to be capable of a better understanding considering their football abilities. It really seems as if there will be none of the old running, passing and scoring until the old firm of Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son and Harris are at work once more. Fortunately they are all recovering, and should be seen together before the season is out.

Among the Warrington men, chief praise is due to Berry who played a magnificent defensive game at full back and earned high praise .from the Hull people. True he was robbed by West for the first try, but he could hardly be blamed. The half backs also played smartly, Hockenhalll being ever to the fore, but the handling broke down badly in the third line. Here Rees and Phipps quite overplayed Taylor and Jones. The latter is hardly class enough for senior company and requires some development of speed and dash to carry his ideas into operation. Hard work on the running path in the close season would do a lot towards bringing him out.

There is no chi:taco of seeing Warrington at borne before March 10th when Wigan are the visitors. To- day they go to Barrow, on Tneiday to Braiford, anti on March 3rd to Batley.

THE JUDGE WITHDRAWS.

His Lordship, addressing Mr. Talbot, said he had no doubt that in the past he had performed his work well. The letter which he had written to the solicitor for the defence showed that he did all he ought to have d ) no, and so far as his (the judge's) comments on the previous day were concerned he had much pleasure in withdrawing thorn entirely as far as the Chief Constable was concerned. He however,left his remarks where they were with the exception that he did not attach the Chief Constable's name to them. The case was more extraordinary- now than ever it was, and it only showed how careful judges ought to be in doing what he always tried to do—to get to the bottom of every- thing. He never know a case in which there had been greater necessity for closer investigation before the magistrates than in that case. There was not the slightest reference in the depositions to the statement, and there was not the slightest suspicion that such'a document existed. There was everything in the case likely to create a miscarriage of justice, and the extraordinary part of the thing was that there had been a committal at all. If he (the judge) had known of the existence of such a document at first he would have directed the grand jury to throw out the bill. The Chief Constable said he had begged that the statement should be put on the depositions in both the coroner's and the magistrates' court. He did not want a committal for he knew prisoners had been persecuted, and were the victims of intense malice. Mr. Talbot I finally returned thanks to his Lordship for having cleared his reputation.

LETTER FROM THE CHIEF CONSTABLE

a A Drama of Misunderstandings." To 'film EDITOR, " EXAMINER." Sir,—Will you permit me to refer briefly in your valued columns to the singular circumstances under which in the case of Rex v. Drury and Waterworth at Liverpool Assizes, His Lordship, Justice Grantham , (through a misapprehension) passed on my conduct a censure as emphatic as it would have been deserved had I been guilty of the mal-practice attributed to me. The learned Judge called it" iniquitous." I should describe such enact even more strongly and term it "criminal." a form of criminality which of RII others I hold in abhorrence, viz :—the suppression or distortion of evidence affecting grave issues to accused persons. Humbly and heartily I concur with the learned Judge's abstract condemnation—its particular application to myself was. when I appeared before him on the follow- ing day, withdrawn as thoroughly, publicly and unequi- vocally as it had been uttered. With new light on

matters his Lordship turned his censure into a compli- ment with that rare grace, tact and frankness so characteristic of this eminent Judge. I trust it is no presumption to make the latter observation. This fortunate culmination was led to by the energy, ability and good feeling of nay friend Mr. Steel (solicitor), jointly with the lucid eloquence of Mr. Madden (counsel for the defence), whose address to the jury for the accused is despribed as the oratorical feature of this Assize. Havidg heard him nest day in a few powerful sentences dispel the foul miasma which had for some hours wrapped ray unhappy but innocent head, lam quite ready to believe that this compliment was no flattery. To these two gentlemen acting :spontane- ously on my behalf, from their sense of honour and fair play, I feel most deeply indebted. I must also thank Mr. Starkey, of the Town Clerk's department, who also worked hard to put the wrong right. For myself I blame no one—feel bitterly towards no one—in the matter. It was a little drama of mis- understandings in which (willy-nilly) I was cast for the role of " Villain," but as it did not run more than one night (a sleepless one), I content myself with musing upon the axioms—slightly altered—" All's well that ends better," and "A Policeman's lot is-1 bit mixed." Thanking you in anticipation, I am, Dear Sir, faithfully yours,— _

LUKE TALBOT, (Chief Constable.)

Board of Guardians.

A meeting of this Board was held on Wednesday under the presidency of Mr. A. J. Jolley. There were also present—Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broad- bent, Rev. Dr. Mercer, Messrs.G. Thompson, S.Davies, G. Mather, M. W. Seton, R. Flannagan, Vook, R. Forshaw, S. ;%tton (assistant clerk), J. F. twbery (master) and A. Wright (superintendent of i'xlgate schools). The assistant clerk informed the Board that two recommendations of increases of salaries to officials would be placed on the agenda for the next Board meeting. Miss Broadbent said she desired through the Press to thank the tradespeople who had been most generous in their support of the Brabazon sale of work. A good sum had been realized by the sale of tickets and they were much indebted to Mr. Awbery for his valuable efforts.—The assistant clerk announced that the annual audit of the acczunts of the Union would take place on March 12th. He formally laid before the Board the estimates for the half year ending September next which will be fully reported upon at a future meeting. We understand that the estimates show a decreased deficiency as compared with the previous half- ydar of from £l,OOO to This is equal roughly to a reduction of id. In the rates of the Union.

===== 0002601_19060224_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
boys and girls ought to have some form of Bible teach- lug he would be prepared—until the country was ripe for the only real and logical solution of the whole difficulty —to say Amen to what the Bishop ofCarlisle so distinctly advocated. If their Anglican friends would only take the same view as that held by theßishop of Carlisle, then for the time being at any rate,the .religions controversy would be stayed. At the same time compromise was never a safe solution of any difficulty, and if a com- promise was arrived at they must remember it would only be temporary, and that sooner or later a fair, full , and final solution would be demanded, and to him the only logical solution, as he had said, was the absolute secularisation of the schools. That would mean that teachers would not be compelled to teach what some of them did not believe ; that the schools would not only be completely under State control, but that their teaching being free for sectarian controvery and bitterness would become more and more efficient; that men and women, whether Catholic or Protestant, would have equal opportunity in the schools of the people and, he hoped it would mean that the churches would see that religion was a spiritual thing and that they and they alone were specifically called to minister in the things of the Lord not only to men and women but to boys and girls as well. (Applause). A considerable discussion ensued in which the opinion was expressed (by Mr. Griffiths) that the secularisation of the schools would be dangerous as it would lend to an increase in atheism, and, by several members, that a solution of the question was to open the schools during certain hours of the day for religious instruction, to be given by the clergy, the attendance of the teachers and of the scholars not to be made compulsory. Replying to the discussion Mr. Matthews said that when the Clergy claimed the schools as their property they were not strictly correct. Many of the schools could not have been built without the aid of Nonconformist contributions and moreover large grants had been received from the State to aid in the building of tie schools. They must remember that the Anglicans and the Roman Catholics did not want facilities for denom- inational instruction to be given after school hours. They wanted religious instruction be given at the expense of the State, in State schools, and in State time. He ventured to suggest that the present position of secularism in England and the hostility of a great number of men to religion was largely due the cast iron creeds which they were compelled to learn in their childhood. Their intellects as they had grown to man- hood had refused to be, cribbed, cabinecl and confined by those creeds, the teaching of which was very largely in the hands of Anglican and Roman Catholic Churches,. It was because those things had been drummed into them as boys that many had altogether revolted against religion later in life. He ventured to think there would be nothing like the amount of secularism which mani- fested itself to-day if the Bible were to be abolished from the schools and the Churches themselves rose to, the occasion and to the effective discharge of their important responsibilities and used their persuasive powers to inculcate their various religion tenets in the minds of the people. The religion train- ing of the nation ought to take place in the home, the Sunday school, and the Church. The first and the essential principle of Free Churchrnanship was that the State had no right to interfere in any way with religion Let them get that principle rooted and grounded in the minds of the people and they could expect for religion a free and fair course and when the people saw that religion was absolutely dissociated from anything in the nature of compulsion or force it seemed to him that they would accept it for what it was and if it spoke of truth, freedom, and goodwill, would use it for those purposes for which it was designed. (Applause). The Rev. C. L. STOWE said he must confess that to him it was not such a terrible thing as it was to many to take the Bible away from the schools altogether.. The burden of religious instruction should rest upon the shoulders of the Church and the home. (Applause). The meeting closed with the pronouncing of the Benediction.

_ Sunday School Teachers' Association - - Quarterly Meeting. -

"We, the parents of the Catholic children attend- ing St. Mary's Roman Catholic School, Warrington, recognizing that tho duty and responsibility of edu- cating our children belongs to us and to us alone, deny the right of any power to take duty out of our hands, or even to help in the discharge of it, unless this be done in entire conformity with our con- scientious convictions." He entirely agreed with that resolution. It was essentially the Nonconformist position. They all believed that their children should be trained in their own principles. They all believed that that training should be given by themselves and resented any attempt to take it out of their hands. They would notice, thac the resolution said nothing about that being done at the expense of the state. In their second resolution however the Catholics said : "We, the parents of the Catholic children attend- ing St. Mary's Roman Catholic School, Warrington, who number over 900, regarding our schools, built at the cost of enormous sacrifice, as a sacred trust to be defended and preserved intact, hereby pledge our- selves to resist to the utmostany legislation or action of the Government which takes away from Cathqlics the right to have for their children Catholic religions instruction during school hours, given by Catholic teachers only, in Catholic schools, and under Catholic management, or that would impose any but Catholic teachers on the regular staff of our schools." The crucial point of that resolution lay in the fact that those particular schools were supported by the whole body of ratepayers, received Government grants, and were still to be made a place where specific Roman Catholic teaching should be given, by specially trained Roman Catholic teachers, with a ICatholic atmosphere, and given during school hours at the expense of the state. He supposed that would be the great question with which the present Government would have to deal, and it was tie crucial point round which centred the whole of the controversy on religious education. There were practically three theories as to the kind and quality of the training which ought to be given to the boys and girls in our schools. The first was that advocated by their Roman Catholic friends and a section of the Anglican clergy who held that even- though schools might be under popular control the atmosphere of the schools should be essentially the , atmosphere of the Church under whose trust deed the particular schools existed, and that the religious teachiug shouldlbe:theteaching of that particular church. There was the second theory, held by a large body of Nonconformists and held also by a large body of Angli- cans that in schools under public management the teach- ing should be purely Bible teaching—teaching on the basis of the Oxford and Cambridge local examinations in that particular subject : The third theory was to have absolutely no religious teaching at all. He wanted to ray to their Anglican and Roman friends at the time was not far distant when willy-nilly they would be compelled to recognize that it was both inequitable and unjust for anything in the nature of denominational teaching at the expense of the whole body of rate- payers or of the State, to be given in the schools of the State. He ventured to suggest that it was equally unjust for what was called interdenominational or undo- nominational or purely Bible teaching to be given at the expense of the State. He was aware that on that point he differed from a very largo number of the Non- conformists but it seemed to him that it was just as much inequitable to give purely Bible teaching at the expense of the State as it was to teach the more formal and complicated theology of the Roman Catholic and Anglican Churches. Two of the prin- ciples for which they as Free Churchmen existed were that it was not the function of the State to interfere with religion and that it was not the function of the State to teach 9,ny one form or any particular forms of religion and if seemed to him inyiew of those principles very unjust to teach any one form of religion. Someone said" What about the poor children? If you don't teach the Bible in the schools there are hundreds and thousands of boys and girls who will never hear of Jesus Christ; who will know precious little about God and whose characters therefore will not be strengthened and nourished." He recognized the serious- ness of the difficulty. But was it a reason why the State should give distinctive teaching, even though parents were safeguarded by the con- science clause, just because thousands of chil- dren would not have religious teaching at all? He ventured to suggest that for the Chnrat to allow the State to undertake religious training, or for the Church to have made it possible for the question of religious training or the absence of religious training to be raised at all was to permit a kind of libel to be launched against her good faith, her ability and her organization. If it came to the question unless the State taught the children hundreds and thousands would not receive any religions education, then, he said, so much the worse for the Churches. But he refused to believe that that was the only alternative. They had their massive Free Church organization ready to- morrow to step to the front and to bring all its forces to gather the children together at specific times and in specific places to receive regular and systematic Bible instruction. He was inclined to think that if their Anglican and Roman Catholic friends per- sisted in their demands to keep in their own hands the management of their schools and to create in them, at the expense of the ratepayers, an atmosphere of the pa:Similar Church to which they belonged, then there was nothing for it but to secularize the schools altogether. That to him was the most logical and the most consistent as well as being the best solution of the whole difficulty, for it would at one rem aye fro a the spheretof the elementary school all sectarian controversy and bitterness. That he ventured to think would be a great gain. For not only would the educational efficiency , of the schools be vastly improved but it would be a far happier augury for religion. It would help to promote the unity of churches and would cause them to strain every effort to increase their organization where neces- sary Si) that there would be no boy or girl throughout the land who did not receive districtinr(eeildigiocuiserignsytrauncd- ' Hen. But there were still fair ni Bishops who rather than have purely secular instruction given in the schools, were prep mad to advocate un- denerninationall teaching. The Bishop writing to " Times " suggested two alternatives—Secularism and nnclenominational education supplemented, he hoped one or more mornings every week by facilities for denominational instruction. He (Mr. Matthews) most cordially accepted the latter alterna- tive. While he did not, believe in religious instruction in the schools, for the sake of those who believed that

Guild Sunday at Bawsey-road. Special services were large in Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday the day being observed as Guild Sunday. The preacher throughout the day was the Rev. C. H. Hocken, of Liverpool, a former and popular mi.iister of the Warrington Circuit, who at each service addressed largo congregations. A musical service was held in the afternoon at which there was a crowded congregation. Alderman TisNrox, who presided, said he was exceed- ingly pleased that the Wesley Guild had so far pro- gressed as to see fit to have services entirely dedicated to it. He believed it was an institution which did a great amount of good and was deserving of the heartiest sympathy and help of them all. Before the establish-- ment of the Guild the link was missing which should connect Methodist children from the Sunday school with the Church, just at the age in the lives of the young people when such connection was most necessary. At a critical age in the lives of their children things were left to chance. There was nothing to help them to continue their association with Methodism, but by the formation of the Crili ld that difficulty had ceased to exist. The Guild deserved their heartiest support. because it provided the missing. link and took charge of the spiritual and moral welfare of their boys and girls, and also took in hand another departineat which was often too much neglected—the very proper in en t itsandw a aunthealthy w as recreation,one of those Recreation was in life in which the Church, in his opinion, ought to take a stronger and a more leading part. During the time he was in \Farrington, Mr. Hocken took an active interest in the Guild, and paid special attention to the social side with considerable success. Appreciating the value of the Guild, he was very pleased to take the chair that afternoon, and to do what little he could to stimulate the work on that department of Methodism. The Rev. C. EL HOCK, who was accorded a warm, reception, spoke upon the common conception of God. The general idea was, he said, that He was something of the nature of a schoolmaster and a magistrate. RELIGIOUS INSTRUCTION IN DAY SCHOOLS. THE NONCONFORMIST ATTITUDE.

THE REV. J. F MATTHEWS AND THE SOLUTION OF THE DIFFICULTY.

A lengthy and vigorous address on the Education question was delivered by the Rev. J. F. Matthews at the meeting of the Warrington Young Free Churchmen's Auxiliary, which was held on Wednesday evening at the Bold-street Wesleyan Chapel. The Rev. C. L. Stowe presided. The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS said he was delighted to see that Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman in his address on the King's speech, referred to the question ot educa- tion, when he said the patrons of the present system. were as much responsible for the religious controversy in connection with education as those who protested against it. He would venture to change one word in the Prime Minister's remark, and say that the patrons of the present system were more responsible than those who protested, and protested with so much force against it. Even since the early part of the 19th Century, when an attempt had been made by the Government to introduce a national system of education, the Bishops and the Clergy of the Anglican Church had fought against it except where a national system had been associated with the teaching of the Church of England. The whole of the controversy on the Educa- tion question was complicated by the question of its association with the teaching of religion or theology. It seemed a great pity that in England we had not yet realised that religion was spiritual and that the per- suasive enforcement of its principles should be left to the Churches and that the state schools should not have their usefulness threatened by a controversy as to what particular form of religious teaching should be given therein. In his judgment the whole question of the religious education of children was not a parent's ques- tion but a parson's question. He said that advisedly and respectfully though with some degree of reserva- tion. No body of people in England as yet asked that in the elementary schools their children should be taught, at the expense of the State, those specific prin- ciples for which their particular church stood. He mad 6 a reservation in respect to that statement as he noticed that the Roman Catholic parents of Warrington had passed some very strong resolutions. The first was:

(From. the Warrington Examiner, February 10th, 1906. ebe Zuilbing : "Architecturally the building is a distinct ornament to the street, and can be classed amongst the most striking erections in the town. It has been effectively carried out in the French Renaissance style, and the whole of the front is faced with glazed terra cotta. The premises are thoroughly adapted to the firm's rapidly increasing require- ments, and are fitted up in the most modern style. The cased-in windows are quite a new departure in ‘Varringtdn, the glass-work at the back of each bearing the Warrington Borough Arms and the Manchester City Arms respectively. The electroliers with which the windows and the whole of the establishment are illuminated make a very fine display." Ebe Zugitte66 : "It is certainly an advantage that the various sections of the firm's bnsiness can be carried out in one building under the supervision of the local manager (Mr. Harvey.) Hitherto the [inn has confined its attentions chiefly to gent's tailoring, for which it has achieved a high reputation, but in the new shop a special feature is the ladies depart- ment. A handsome staircase leads to the lofty and splendidly arranged show-room for ladies' costumes, and adjoining it is a fitting-room." cLanitarp tElorkoboo : "The workshop is one of the best adapted in the north of England, and the heating and ventilation arrangements are all that could be desired. TO-DAY'S RECIPE. CRAYFISH PUDDING.—Take a dozen crayfish and place them in a stew-pan with sufficient water to cover them, and cook for five minutes, after which remove the flesh and place it on one tido. Then crush the shells in a mortar with of butter, place in a saucepan on the fire, and stir till the butter becomes red. Pour in a little loss than a pint of water, and set aside in a bowl to eoitl. When cold, the red butter should be removed—reserving a little fQr the sauce— and mixed with 14oz. of the crumb of a loaf which is at least two days old, moistened with thout a cupful of milk. Then stir in the yolks of three eggs and 2oz. of castor sugar, the flesh of the crayfish finely minced, a little salt, 3oz. of thopped suet, and, at the last, the whites of the taws beaten stiffly. Let the pudding boil for wo hours anti a-half to throo hours in a well- floured cloth, and serve with a sauce made with crayfish butter, as above, flour and milk, with the addition of a little cream and cayenne pepper.

The usual meeting of this Society was held on Sunday, when there was a good attendance. Mr. Robins presided. and spoke upon " The power of goodness from an historical standpoint." Mr. Luckman, of Manchester, was the speaker, his subject being "Foot passengers : Keep to the right." He warned the members against gambling and drink as great drawers to the left. Mr. Nadin, of Ashton-under-Lyne, recited with musical accompaniment "If we only knew" and " The two waifs" with good effect. Mr. J. Owen and Mr. W. I Beswick also took part in the meeting. Mr. W. H. Leigh's remaining matches are THE CHIEF CONSTABLE. with Morecambe, Huddersfield, and Oldham at home, and with Millom, Barrovr,St. Helens,Rochdale Hornets, Huddersfield, and Hull Kingston Rovers away. The REMARKABLE INCIDENT AT last-named are the only Yorkshire club that Leigh have yet met this season, but a Leigh correspondent LIVERPOOL ASSIZES. states that the Mather-lane organization sought and were refused fixtures with such clubs as Hunslet, Halifax, Leeds, and Bradford. Clearly if Leigh should A Censure and a Withdrawal. eventually be the League champions—it would be unfair, as the correspondent points out, to find fault THE CONGEALMENT OF BIRTH CHARGE. with the fortunate club on account of their paucity of Yorkshire engagements. Rather should criticism be levelled at the League itself under which such an unequal system of arranging matches is possible. --- Many people thought that the "A "team had put up a record for the Warrington Club when they scored IS goals, 12 tries, 52 points against Heyside, the junior Oldham club, at Wilderspool on Saturday. This is not the fact, however, the score against Rugby still standing as the local record. The match was played at Warrington on December 30th, 1893, the score being 10 goals, three tries, 59 ',points to nil. Barber made his first appearance in this match and had a lot to do with the total. In the more recent cup-tie with Goole, at Wilderspool, Warrington scored 7 goals, 10 tries, 44 points, of which Fish obtained 7 goals, 5 tries. Some very fine football was seen in the aforesaid match with Hey-side. The visitors, though hopelessly outclassed, showed plenty of pluck and opened the game out in the most approved style. They could do I nothing with Dickinson, who was like a giant among pigmies and displayed form of the highest class at centre. There were some brilliant passing runs, Lloyd and Lambert setting the threequarters going at 'every opportunity. The last-named, for such a midget, played a wonderful game, and the way he tricked man tafter man at full speed delighted the crowd. It was a I source of general regret that he got hurt in the last five minutes. With luck, he should make a second Tot " Jones behind the scrum. Warrington have by no means an easy task to-day, when they play Barrow at Cavendish Bank. Though they beat the Furness men easily enough by 18 points at Wilderspool, this does not make success a certainty on the Barrow ground. There is no doubt that War- rington ought to win, but they will have to put in all they know, and bear in mind the fact that Wigan sns- tabled an unexpected reverse at Barrow a fortnight ago. -- I am glad to see Dickenson in the premier team once more, and his presence should help a lot towards pull- ing the three-quarter line together. The other change from last week is Jolley for Shugars. The team to play Barrow is :—Berry, full back ; Kenyon, Dicken- son, Taylor, and Widdesen, three-quareers ; Hocken-1 hall and Lees, half backs ; Preston, Boardman, ' Thomas, Herta), Harmer, Belton, Swift and Jolley, forwards. Another gratifying event this week is the re-appear ance of Isherwood, who is indulging in a trial run with the" A" team against Runcorn Fertilisers. The selected • twelve are —Knight;: Jordan, Isherwood, Cartwright and Jones; Lambert and Lloyd; Forster, Fairhurst, Bradshaw, Morrison and Naylor. I hear excellent reports, too, of Fish. He has got rid of his crutches, and means to get himself in fit condition at the earliest moment. There is just an outside chance of Fish, Isherwood and Dickenson all playing against Batley, though this is rather short notice for the." irrepressible." Lancashire and Cumberland met on Wednesday at Wigan to decide the county championship of the Northern Union. The earlier matches in this very limited competition had left these two bracketed for the distinction. An attendance of 5,000 people watched a game that was uninterestingly full of scrummages. Just before half-time Kitchen got a try 1 from Cumberland,and in the second half Harry scored for Lancashire. Neither of the phice-kicks was suc- cessful, neither side scored again, and so the result was a draw of three points each. The rules for this season's Works Competition have now been drawn up and printed, and copies may be obtained from Mr. H. Ashton. The Warrington Club again provides jerseys and pants for competing teams There is no truth in the rumour of a junction between the Northern Union and the leading Welsh clubs. The matter has certainly been informally die cussed by the Welsh clubs, but there is no likelihood of them joining the professional ranks.
